F:=F^{A}
and
G^{\Sigma}:=G\big|_{\Sigma}
. Then (away from
\Sigma
) the
Bianchi identity gives

0=\sigma^{\alpha+1}\nabla_{[a}F_{bc]}=-\alpha n_{[a}G_{bc]}+{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma)

and thus, along
\Sigma
,

\hat{n}_{[a}G^{\Sigma}_{bc]}=0\,.

Moreover

(4.9)

j[A]=\sigma^{-\alpha}\big(-\alpha n^{a}G_{ab}-(d-4)n^{a}G_{ab}+{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma)\big)=0\,,

so that, along
\Sigma
,

(\alpha+d-4)\,\hat{n}^{a}G^{\Sigma}_{ab}=0\,.

There is no non-vanishing solution to the above two displays for
G^{\Sigma}
unless
\alpha=4-d
.
∎

Observe, that the above result matches the behavior in Equation (
4.3
) given in the characteristic exponent analysis at the beginning of Section
4
.

The next lemma shows that the boundary data for Problem
1.3
is encoded by an “electric field” subject to a non-abelian Gauß law (Equation (
4.11
)).

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
\nabla^{A}
be a connection on a
d\geq 5
conformally compact structure
(M,\boldsymbol{c},\sigma)
with boundary condition
\bar{\nabla}^{\bar{A}}
.
If

j[A]={\mathcal{O}}(\sigma^{\infty})\quad\mbox{ and }\quad F^{A}=\sigma^{d-4}G\,,

for some smooth
G\in\Gamma(\wedge^{2}M\otimes\operatorname{End}{\mathcal{V}}M[4-d])
,
then
G^{\Sigma}:=G\big|_{\Sigma}
must obey

(4.10)

G^{\Sigma}_{ab}=\hat{n}_{a}E_{b}-\hat{n}_{b}E_{a}\,,

where
E\in\Gamma(T^{*}\Sigma\otimes\operatorname{End}{\mathcal{V}}\Sigma[3-d])
is subject to

(4.11)

\bar{\nabla}^{{\bar{A}}}_{a}E^{a}=0\,.

Proof.

We need to study the
\sigma^{d-4}{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma)
terms in
j[A]
that were not computed in Equation (
4.9
) in the proof of Lemma
4.6
. These are

j_{b}[A]=\sigma^{d-3}\nabla^{a}G_{ab}\,.

Now, because
\hat{n}_{[a}G_{bc]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}0
, it follows that

G^{\Sigma}_{ab}=\hat{n}_{a}E_{b}-\hat{n}_{b}E_{a}\,,

where
E\in\Gamma(T^{*}\Sigma\otimes\operatorname{End}{\mathcal{V}}\Sigma[3-d])
. Note that

E_{b}=G^{\Sigma}_{\hat{n}b}
because
\hat{n}^{a}E_{a}=0
. Using the result of the display before last, dividing by
\sigma^{d-3}
in the interior, then extending to
\Sigma
and contracting with a unit conormal yields

0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\hat{n}^{b}\nabla^{a}G_{ab}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\hat{n}^{b}(\nabla^{a}-\hat{n}^{a}\nabla_{\hat{n}})G_{ab}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\hat{n}^{b}(\nabla^{a}-\hat{n}^{a}\nabla_{\hat{n}})(\hat{n}_{a}E_{b}-\hat{n}_{b}E_{a})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}-\bar{\nabla}_{a}E^{a}\,.

Here we used antisymmetry of
G
to achieve the
second equality.
The third equality used that the operator
\nabla^{a}-\hat{n}^{a}\nabla_{\hat{n}}
is tangential.
For the fourth we used that

\hat{n}^{b}(\nabla_{a}-\hat{n}_{a}\nabla_{\hat{n}})\hat{n}_{b}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\hat{n}^{a}E_{a}
.
∎

Remark 4.8
.

It is easy to see that there exist connections
A_{0}
satisfying

F^{A_{0}}=\sigma^{d-4}G
where
G
obeys Equations (
4.10
) and (
4.11
). For example, in the Fefferman–Graham expansion explained in Section
7.1
, one can take the connection coefficients to vanish up until order
r^{d-3}
, with then the coefficient
E/(d-3)
.

\blacklozenge

Given a connection
\nabla^{A}
, whose curvature obeys
F^{A}=\sigma^{d-4}G
,
the data
E_{b}=\hat{n}^{a}G^{\Sigma}_{ab}\in\Gamma(T^{*}\Sigma\otimes\operatorname{End}{\mathcal{V}}\Sigma[3-d])

may be viewed
as the boundary values of a non-abelian “electric field”. Indeed, Lemma
4.7

shows that
the electric field
E
of
solutions to
Problem
1.3

must obey the Gauß law (
4.11
).
We are now ready to prove Theorem
1.4

which gives a recursive solution to Problem
1.3
for connections with boundary electric data.

Proof of Theorem
1.4
.

The proof mimics that of Theorem
1.2
.
From Lemmas
4.6
and
4.7
and the boundary conditions in the statement of the theorem,
it follows that

j[A_{0}]=\sigma^{d-3}k^{(1)}

for some
k^{(1)}\in\Gamma(T^{*}M\otimes\operatorname{End}{\mathcal{V}}M[2-d])
. The power
d-3
of
\sigma
in the above display is precisely the value for which
the system of equations (
4.1
) degenerates
and thus does not imply that
\hat{n}^{a}k_{a}^{(1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}0
. Nonetheless, this condition still holds by virtue of
the computation presented in the last display proof of Lemma
4.7
combined with the fact that
the boundary electric field obeys the Gauß Law (
4.11
). Thus the recursion used to
prove Theorem
1.2
can be applied at all orders since Equation (
4.6
) is needed only for values of
\ell\geq d-2
.
∎

We can use the methods of the above proof to prove the all orders result of Theorem
1.5
. This gives a smooth to the boundary, asymptotic solution to the magnetic problem
1.1
in the case that
the obstruction current
\bar{k}
vanishes and additional electric field boundary data is supplied.

Proof of Theorem
1.5
.

Because the obstruction current
\bar{k}=0
we have that

j[A_{0}]=\sigma^{d-3}k^{(1)}\,,

for some
k^{(1)}\in\Gamma(T^{*}M\otimes\operatorname{End}{\mathcal{V}}M[2-d])
.
This order is precisely encountered in the proof of Theorem (
1.2
) where the corresponding right hand side of the above display is
not
forced to obey
\hat{n}^{a}k^{(1)}_{a}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}0
.
To achieve a condition on
\hat{n}^{a}k_{a}^{(1)}
, we must partially use the undetemined-ness of the data
L
(see Proposition
4.5
for another instance of this). Thus
we now extend Equation (
4.5
)
to include terms of order
\sigma^{d-3}

j_{b}[A_{0}+\sigma^{d-3}L]\!=\sigma^{d-3}k^{(1)}_{b}+\sigma^{d-3}n^{a}(\nabla_{a}L_{b}-\nabla_{b}L_{a})+(d-3)\sigma^{d-3}\nabla^{a}(n_{a}L_{b}-n_{b}L_{a})+{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma^{d-2})\,.

Here we have denoted by
\nabla
the connection
\nabla^{A_{0}}
.
Contracting the above with
n^{b}
gives

(4.12)

n^{b}j_{b}[A_{0}+\sigma^{d-3}L]=\sigma^{d-3}n^{b}k^{(1)}_{b}+(d-3)\sigma^{d-3}n^{b}\nabla^{a}(n_{a}L_{b}-n_{b}L_{a})+{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma^{d-2})\,,

where we used that
n^{a}n^{b}(\nabla_{a}L_{b}-\nabla_{b}L_{a})=0
.
Now define

E^{\rm ext}_{a}=\sqrt{n^{2}-\frac{2\sigma}{d}(\Delta^{g}\sigma+J\sigma)}\,L_{a}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}|n|_{g}L_{a}\,.

The factor under the square root is a conformally invariant extension of
|n|_{g}\big|_{\Sigma}
to
M
(indeed it equals
\sqrt{-\frac{Sc^{{\mathring{g}}}}{d(d-1)}}{\hskip 0.85358pt}
; see Section
6.1
for further discussion of this point), so
E^{\rm ext}\in\Gamma(T^{*}M\otimes\operatorname{End}{\mathcal{V}}M[3-d])
. Now, since
L
only appears in the combination
n_{a}L_{b}-n_{b}L_{a}

in Equation (
4.12
),
we may further assume that
L
, and hence
E^{\rm ext}
are both extensions of sections of
T^{*}\Sigma\otimes\operatorname{End}{\mathcal{V}}\Sigma[3-d]
so that
\hat{n}^{a}E_{a}^{\rm ext}\big|_{\Sigma}=0
where
E=E^{\rm ext}\big|_{\Sigma}
. Thus

n^{b}\nabla^{a}(n_{a}L_{b}-n_{b}L_{a})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}n^{b}\nabla^{a}{}^{\top}\big(\hat{n}_{a}E_{b}^{\rm ext}-\hat{n}_{b}E_{a}^{\rm ext}\ \big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}-|n|\nabla^{a}{}^{\top}E_{a}^{\rm ext}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}-|n|\bar{\nabla}_{a}E^{a}\,.

Using that the electric field
E
obeys the Gauß law (
4.11
) we now have that

j[A_{0}+\sigma^{d-3}L]=\sigma^{d-3}j^{(1)}\,,

for some
j^{(1)}\in\Gamma(T^{*}M\otimes\operatorname{End}{\mathcal{V}}M[2-d])
such that

\hat{n}^{a}j^{(1)}_{a}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}0
.
The remainder of the proof follows that of Theorem
1.2
,
mutatis mutandis
.
∎

Remark 4.9
.

In the case that the obstruction current
\bar{k}
vanishes (a special case of which includes the purely electric solution to Problem
4.3
), we have established the existence of a smooth all order asymptotic solution. This depends on the data of a boundary connection
\bar{\nabla}
and a boundary electric field
E\in\Gamma(T^{*}\Sigma\otimes\operatorname{End}{\mathcal{V}}\Sigma[3-d])
whose divergence is constrained by Equation (
4.11
). However, we do not expect the data
(\bar{\nabla},E)
to be independent in the case of a global solution, but rather—viewing these respectively as Dirichlet and Neumann data—they ought be related by a (non-linear) Dirichlet-to-Neumann map; see Section
7.3
.

\blacklozenge

5.
Poincaré–Einstein Manifolds

A key problem handled by our methods is the study of the Yang–Mills equations on Poincaré–Einstein manifolds.
Here we gather technical results for Poincaré–Einstein structures and their
asymptotically Yang–Mills connections.
Recall that a conformally compact structure
(M,\boldsymbol{c},\sigma)
is called
Poincaré–Einstein
when
the singular metric, defined on
M_{+}=M\backslash\Sigma
by

{\mathring{g}}=\sigma^{-2}{\mbox{\boldmath$g$}}\,,

has vanishing trace-free Schouten tensor

P^{{\mathring{g}}}_{(ab)_{\circ}}=0\,.

The moniker
asymptotically Poincaré–Einstein
is used when

(5.1)

P^{{\mathring{g}}}_{(ab)_{\circ}}={\mathcal{O}}(\sigma^{d-3})\,.

It is useful to rewrite the above condition in a
way that extends to the boundary
\Sigma
.
A standard computation on
M^{+}
shows that

P^{{\mathring{g}}}_{(ab)_{\circ}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{=}}\sigma^{-1}\big(\nabla_{(a}\nabla_{b)_{\circ}}+P_{(ab)_{\circ}}\big){\hskip 0.85358pt}\sigma\,.

Multiplying this by
\sigma
we have that
(M,\boldsymbol{c},\sigma)
is asymptotically Poincaré–Einstein when

(5.2)

\nabla_{a}n_{b}+\sigma P_{ab}+g_{ab}\rho={\mathcal{O}}(\sigma^{d-2})\,.

Here
n:={\rm d}\sigma
and

\rho:=-\frac{1}{d}(\Delta+J)\sigma
. Importantly the left hand side of the above display extends smoothly to the boundary
\Sigma
.

The scalar curvature of
{\mathring{g}}
can be also expressed in terms of
g
and
n
, indeed

(5.3)

Sc^{{\mathring{g}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{=}}-d(d-1)\big(|n|_{g}^{2}+2\rho\sigma\big)\,.

This shows that the scalar curvature of the singular metric
extends smoothly to the boundary.
The above display implies that the Poincaré–Einstein metric
{\mathring{g}}
has
negative
constant scalar curvature; by convention we choose the normalization
Sc^{{\mathring{g}}}=-d(d-1)
.

The conformal boundary embedding
\Sigma\hookrightarrow(M,\boldsymbol{c})
for a Poincaré–Einstein structure must be umbilic
[
20
,
39
]
, meaning that the trace-free part
\mathring{{\bf\rm I\hskip-0.56905ptI}}{\hskip 0.56905pt}_{ab}
of the second fundamental form
vanishes.
The following lemma gives identities for the boundary embedding of a Poincaré–Einstein structure,
many of which follow directly from the umbilic property.
Note that bars are used to indicate boundary quantities so, for example,
\bar{P}_{ab}
is the boundary Schouten tensor for the Levi-Civita connection
\bar{\nabla}
of the induced boundary metric
\bar{g}_{ab}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}g_{ab}-\hat{n}_{a}\hat{n}_{b}
. Also, we use the same abstract indices to denote
boundary tensors, relying on the Gauß isomorphism between
T\Sigma
and
(TM|_{\Sigma})^{\perp}
where
\perp
denotes the bundle projection orthogonal to the unit conormal
\hat{n}
.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
d\geq 3
and
(M,\boldsymbol{c},\sigma)
be an asymptotically Poincaré–Einstein structure. Also, let
g\in\boldsymbol{c}
and

\bar{v}^{\rm ext}
be any smooth extension of
\bar{v}\in\Gamma(T^{*}\Sigma)
to
\Gamma(T^{*}M)
. Then

(5.4)

\nabla^{\top}_{a}\bar{v}^{\rm ext}_{b}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\bar{\nabla}_{a}\bar{v}_{b}-H\hat{n}_{b}\bar{v}_{a}\,.

Moreover the following identities hold.

(5.5)

\displaystyle W_{\hat{n}bcd}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle 0\,,

(5.6)

\displaystyle J\>

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle{\bar{J}}+P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}-\frac{d-1}{2}H^{2}\,,

(5.7)

\displaystyle P^{\top}_{ab}\,

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\bar{P}_{ab}-\frac{1}{2}\bar{g}_{ab}H^{2}\,,

(5.8)

\displaystyle\bar{\nabla}_{a}H

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle-P_{\hat{n}a}^{\top}\,,

(5.9)

\displaystyle\bar{\nabla}_{[a}P^{\top}_{b]\hat{n}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle 0\,,

(5.10)

\displaystyle\big(\hat{n}^{a}\nabla_{\hat{n}}P_{ab}\big)^{\top}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\bar{\nabla}_{b}P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}+2H\bar{\nabla}_{b}H\,,

(5.11)

\displaystyle\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}\nabla_{\hat{n}}P_{ab}\>\>

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\nabla_{\hat{n}}J-(d-1)HP_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}+(\bar{\Delta}+\bar{J})H-\tfrac{d}{2}H^{3}\,.

In dimensions
d\geq 4
,

(5.12)

W_{abcd}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}W^{\top}_{abcd}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\bar{W}_{abcd}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d=4}}{{=}}0\,.

When
d\geq 5
, we also have

(5.13)

C_{abc}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}C_{abc}^{\top}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\bar{C}_{abc}\,.

Proof.

These are all specializations of known hypersurface embedding results to Poincaré–Einstein structures.
By umbilicity of the conformal boundary embedding
[
20
,
39
]
we have that

{\rm I\hskip-0.56905ptI}_{ab}=\bar{g}_{ab}H\,.

Recalling that the second fundamental form relates hypersurface and ambient derivatives according to
\nabla^{\top}_{a}\bar{v}^{\rm ext}_{b}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\bar{\nabla}_{a}\bar{v}_{b}-\hat{n}_{b}{\rm I\hskip-0.56905ptI}_{a}^{c}\bar{v}_{c}
, the above display implies
Equation (
5.4
).

Taking a gradient of Equation (
5.2
)
and then skewing over a pair of indices gives

R_{abc\hat{n}}+2\hat{n}_{[a}P_{b]c}+2g_{c[b}\nabla_{a]}\rho\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}0\,.

The (boundary) trace-free part of the above recovers Equation (
5.5
).

Equations (
5.6
-
5.9
) are standard hypersurface identities following directly from the equations of Gauß, Codazzi, Mainardi and Ricci, see for example
[
29
]
, and the vanishing of
\mathring{{\bf\rm I\hskip-0.56905ptI}}{\hskip 0.56905pt}
for Poincaré–Einstein embeddings.
Note also that Equation (
5.9
) is a direct consequence of Equation (
5.8
).

The key to establishing Equation (
5.10
) is to first rewrite
\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{c}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}g^{ac}-\bar{g}^{ac}
and then apply
\nabla^{a}P_{ab}=\nabla_{b}J
. The details are as follows:

\displaystyle\hat{n}^{a}\nabla_{\hat{n}}P_{ab}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma,\top}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\bar{\nabla}_{b}\bar{J}+\bar{\nabla}_{b}P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}-(d-1)H\bar{\nabla}_{b}H-\nabla^{\top}_{a}P^{a}{}_{b}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma,\top}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\bar{\nabla}_{b}\bar{J}+\bar{\nabla}_{b}P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}-(d-1)H\bar{\nabla}_{b}H-\bar{\nabla}^{a}\big(\bar{P}_{ab}-\tfrac{1}{2}\bar{g}_{ab}H^{2}\big)+dH\bar{\nabla}_{b}H

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma,\top}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\bar{\nabla}_{b}P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}+2H\bar{\nabla}_{b}H\,\,.

In the above
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\top}}{{=}}
denotes projection of the index
b
to directions tangential to
\Sigma
.
A similar computation gives Equation (
5.11
):

\displaystyle\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}\nabla_{\hat{n}}P_{ab}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\hat{n}^{a}(g^{bc}-\bar{g}^{bc})\nabla_{c}P_{ab}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\nabla_{\hat{n}}J-\hat{n}^{a}\nabla^{\top}_{b}P_{a}{}^{b}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\nabla_{\hat{n}}J-\hat{n}_{a}\nabla^{\top}_{b}(\bar{P}^{ab}-\tfrac{1}{2}\bar{g}^{ab}H^{2}-\hat{n}^{a}\bar{\nabla}^{b}H-\hat{n}^{b}\bar{\nabla}^{a}H+\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\nabla_{\hat{n}}J+H\bar{J}-\tfrac{d}{2}H^{3}+\bar{\Delta}H-(d-1)HP_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}\,.

Note that, in dimensions
d\geq 4
, the tensor
(d-3)(P^{\top}_{ab}-\bar{P}_{ab}+H\mathring{{\bf\rm I\hskip-0.56905ptI}}{\hskip 0.56905pt}_{ab}-\frac{1}{2}\bar{g}_{ab}H^{2})
gives a (weight zero) conformal hypersurface invariant
[
17
]
termed the Fialkow tensor in
[
44
,
47
]
. Thus Equation (
5.7
) shows that the Fialkow tensor vanishes for asymptotically Poincaré–Einstein structures. The Fialkow tensor and trace-free second fundamental forms are low-lying examples of the conformal fundamental forms of
[
6
]
which measure obstructions to given conformal embeddings admitting Poincaré–Einstein metrics.
The tensors
W^{\top}_{abcd}
and
\bar{W}_{abcd}
agree when both the Fialkow tensor and trace-free second fundamental form vanish; this gives
Equation (
5.12
).

To establish the second equality in Identity (
5.13
). we compute

\bar{\nabla}_{[a}\bar{P}_{b]c}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\bar{\nabla}_{[a}P_{b]c}^{\top}-\bar{g}_{c[b}H\bar{\nabla}_{a]}H=\big(\nabla^{\top}_{[a}P_{b]c}^{\top}+H\hat{n}_{[b}P_{a]c}^{\top}-\bar{g}_{c[b}H\bar{\nabla}_{a]}H\big)^{\top}=(\nabla_{[a}P_{b]c})^{\top}\,.

The first equality above used Equation (
5.7
) while the second relied on Equation (
5.4
) and that the operation
\top
is a projector.
The last equality employs a combination of Equations (
5.8
) and the fact that for asymptotically Poincaré–Einstein structures we have

(5.14)

\nabla_{a}^{\top}\hat{n}_{b}^{\rm ext}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\bar{g}_{ab}H\,,

for any smooth extension of
\hat{n}
to
M
.
The result then follows from the definition of the Cotton tensor.

It remains to prove the equalities (
5.13
). For that we note the result (see
[
20
]
, for example)

n^{a}W_{abcd}=\sigma C_{cdb}+{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma^{d-3})\,.

This gives an alternative derivation of Equation (
5.5
)
because the Weyl tensor vanishes identically in
d=3
and the right hand side is zero along
\Sigma
when

d\geq 4
. Hooking
n^{b}
into the above display and then taking a normal derivative gives
n^{b}C_{cbd}|_{\Sigma}=0
(when
d\geq 5)
. Remembering that the Cotton tensor obeys
C_{[abc]}=0=C_{(ab)c}
then gives the result.
∎

For many of our computations, the extension of the unit conormal
\hat{n}^{\rm ext}
(the adornment
{\rm ext}
is written as a subscript when the metric is used to produce the corresponding normal vector
\hat{n}^{a}_{\rm ext}
) given by
n=\nabla\sigma
, where
(M,\boldsymbol{c},\sigma)
is an asymptotically Poincaré–Einstein structure,
plays an important
rôle
; the following result gives pertinent identities for this.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
(M^{d},\boldsymbol{c},\sigma)
be an asymptotically Poincaré–Einstein structure.
Then the extension
n_{a}=\nabla_{a}\sigma
of the unit conormal
\hat{n}_{a}
obeys the following identities:

(5.15)

n^{2}=\nabla_{n}\sigma=1-2\rho\sigma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}1\,,

where

\rho:=-\frac{1}{d}(\Delta\sigma+J\sigma)
.

When
d\geq 3

(5.16)

\nabla_{a}n_{b}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}g_{ab}H\,,

(5.17)

\nabla_{n}n_{a}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}H\hat{n}_{a}\,.

When
d\geq 4

(5.18)

\nabla_{\hat{n}}\rho\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}\,,

(5.19)

\nabla_{n}^{2}n_{a}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\big(H^{2}-2P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}})\hat{n}_{a}-P_{\hat{n}a}^{\top}\,.

(5.20)

\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla_{a}n_{b}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}-P_{ab}-g_{ab}P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}\,,

(5.21)

\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}n_{c}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}-\hat{n}_{a}P_{bc}-g_{bc}(P_{\hat{n}a}^{\top}+\hat{n}_{a}P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}})\,.

When
d\geq 5
,

(5.22)

\nabla_{n}^{2}\rho\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}-\bar{\nabla}^{a}P_{\hat{n}a}^{\top}-(d-2)HP_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}+(\nabla_{\hat{n}}+H)J\,,

(5.23)

\nabla_{n}^{3}n_{a}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\Big(H^{3}+(3d-10)HP_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}+3\bar{\nabla}^{a}P_{\hat{n}a}^{\top}-3(\nabla_{\hat{n}}+H)J\Big)\hat{n}_{a}-2\bar{\nabla}_{a}P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}-HP^{\top}_{\hat{n}a}\,.

Proof.

Because
(M,\boldsymbol{c},\sigma)
is asymptotically Poincaré–Einstein, it follows that the scalar curvature
Sc^{\mathring{g}}
equals
-d(d-1)
along
\Sigma
. Hence the first identity in Equation (
5.15
)
follows directly from Equation (
5.3
).

To establish Equation (
5.16
), we note that
Equation (
5.2
) gives

\nabla_{a}n_{b}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}-g_{ab}\rho
, so we need to show that This that

\rho\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}-H
.
This was first proved in
[
20
]
, the argument is as follows:

-\rho\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\frac{1}{d}\Delta\sigma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\frac{1}{d}\big(\nabla_{a}^{\top}n^{a}+\hat{n}_{a}\nabla_{\hat{n}}n^{a}\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\frac{1}{d}\big(\nabla_{a}^{\top}\hat{n}^{a}_{\rm ext}+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{\hat{n}}n^{2}\big)\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\frac{d-1}{d}H+\frac{1}{2d}\nabla_{\hat{n}}\big(1-2\rho\sigma+{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma^{2})\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\frac{d-1}{d}H-\frac{\rho}{d}\,.

In the above display, the first equality used the definition of
\rho

while the second relied on the identity
\nabla_{a}n^{a}=\Delta\sigma
. The third used that
n
is an extension of
\hat{n}
. The fourth equality relied on Equation (
5.14
) and that
n^{2}+2\rho\sigma=1+{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma^{d-1})
. The latter fact can be established by applying the identity
\nabla^{a}P_{ab}=\nabla_{b}J
to Equation (
5.1
). (In fact, as explained in
[
29
]
, it is possible to find an improved defining density
\sigma
that achieves
{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma^{d})
, but this is unimportant here.) The final equality used that
\nabla_{\hat{n}}\sigma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}1
.

Equation (
5.17
) follows directly from Equation (
5.16
). To establish Equation (
5.18
) we compute as follows:

\nabla_{\hat{n}}\rho\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}-\frac{1}{d}\nabla_{\hat{n}}\big(\nabla.n+J\sigma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}-\frac{J}{d}-\frac{1}{d}n^{a}(\nabla_{b}\nabla_{a}n^{b}+R_{ab}{}^{b}{}_{n})\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}-\frac{J}{d}+\frac{1}{d}Ric_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}-\frac{1}{d}n^{a}\Delta n_{a}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\frac{d-2}{d}P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}-\frac{1}{d}\Delta n^{2}+\frac{1}{d}\big[\Delta,n^{a}\big]n_{a}\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\frac{d-2}{d}P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}+\frac{1}{d}\Delta\big(\rho\sigma+{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma^{d-1})\big)+H^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\frac{d-2}{d}P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}+\frac{2}{d}\nabla_{\hat{n}}\rho\,.

The first equality uses the definition of
\rho
, the second relies on
\nabla_{\hat{n}}\sigma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}1
while the third uses that the one-form
n
is exact.
The fourth is the definition of the commutator of operators while the fifth equality follows upon noting that
[\Delta,n_{a}]n^{a}=(\nabla_{b}n_{a})\nabla^{b}n^{a}+\frac{1}{2}\Delta n^{2}
and then applying Equation (
5.16
). The sixth equality uses
\Delta(\rho\sigma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}2\nabla_{\hat{n}}\rho+dH^{2}
and that
\Delta{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma^{d-1})={\mathcal{O}}(\sigma^{d-3})
. The result then follows after some trivial algebra.

To establish Equation (
5.19
) we compute along similar lines:

\nabla_{n}^{2}n_{a}=\nabla_{n}(\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{a}n^{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\!-\nabla_{n}\nabla_{a}(\rho\sigma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\!-\nabla_{a}\rho-\nabla_{n}(n_{a}\rho)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\!\bar{\nabla}_{a}H-\hat{n}_{a}P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}+\hat{n}_{a}H^{2}-\hat{n}_{a}P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}\,.

The result follows upon application of
Equation (
5.8
).

Equations (
5.20
) and (
5.21
) are direct consequences of Equation (
5.2
).
Equation (
5.22
) may be obtained from Lemma 3.10 of
[
28
]
upon specialization to asymptotically Poincaré structures, while for Equation (
5.23
) we calculate as below, again using the same methods as above:

\nabla_{n}^{3}n_{a}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}-\nabla_{n}^{2}\nabla_{a}\big(\rho\sigma+{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma^{d-1})\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}-\nabla_{n}\nabla_{a}\rho-\nabla_{n}((1-2\rho\sigma)\nabla_{a}\rho)-\nabla_{n}^{2}(n_{a}\rho)\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}-2\nabla_{n}\nabla_{a}\rho-2H\nabla_{a}\rho-\hat{n}_{a}H\nabla_{n}\rho-\nabla_{n}((\nabla_{n}n_{a})\rho)-\hat{n}_{a}\nabla_{n}^{2}\rho\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}-2\nabla_{a}\nabla_{n}\rho-2\hat{n}_{a}H\nabla_{n}\rho+H\nabla_{n}^{2}n_{a}-\hat{n}_{a}\nabla_{n}^{2}\rho\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}-2(\bar{\nabla}_{a}+\hat{n}_{a}H)P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}-3\hat{n}_{a}\nabla_{n}^{2}\rho+H\nabla_{n}^{2}n_{a}\,.

The result then follows upon employing Equations (
5.19
) and (
5.22
).

∎

It is also useful to relate the bulk and boundary Laplace operators.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
(M,\boldsymbol{c},\sigma)
be an asymptotically Poincaré–Einstein structure and
g\in\boldsymbol{c}
.
Also let
f\in C^{\infty}M
such that
f|_{\Sigma}=\bar{f}\in C^{\infty}\Sigma
. Then

\Delta f|_{\Sigma}=\bar{\Delta}\bar{f}+\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}f|_{\Sigma}+(d-1)H\nabla_{\hat{n}}f\,.

Proof.

The proof can be found (in a more general context) in
[
28
, Lemma A.2]
.
∎

5.1.
Canonical Expansions

The boundary asymptotics
of a quantity
Q
on a conformally compact structure
(M,\boldsymbol{c},\sigma)
are described by computing

Q_{(k)}
such that

Q=Q_{(k)}+\sigma^{k+1}R=Q_{(k)}+{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma^{k+1})\,,

where
R
is smooth on
M
and
k={\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
. No coordinate choice has been made in the above expression.
Upon picking
coordinates
(x,r)
for a collar neighborhood of the boundary such that
r
itself gives a defining function thereof, one can then study a formal asymptotic series

Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r\to 0}}{{\sim}}Q^{(0)}+Q^{(1)}r\,+Q^{(2)}r^{2}+\cdots\,.

When
Q
is a function, one may ask that the coefficients
Q^{(k)}
are
r
independent. For a canonical
coordinate
r
, the coefficients
Q^{(k)}
are of independent interest. To extract them from
Q_{(k)}
, one picks the scale
g\in\boldsymbol{c}

such that
\sigma=[g;r]
and then computes
\frac{1}{k!}\frac{\partial^{k}Q_{(k)}}{\partial r^{k}}\Big|_{r=0}
in that scale. There exist canonical, conformally invariant boundary operators that perform this computation in rather general settings
[
24
,
6
]
.

In the case
Q
is the singular metric
g^{o}
,
given a choice of boundary metric representative
\bar{h}\in\boldsymbol{c}_{\Sigma}
, there always exists a canonical Graham–Lee normal form
[
33
]
on
M_{+}
such that

(5.24)

{\mathring{g}}=r^{-2}\big({\rm d}r^{2}+h(x,r)\big)\,,

in a collar neighborhood of the boundary
\Sigma
, where

\iota_{\frac{\partial}{\partial r}}h=0

and
h(x,0)=\bar{h}
.
The coordinate
r
is canonical as it measures the normal geodesic distance to the boundary with respect to the compactified metric
g\in\boldsymbol{c}
on
M
given by

(5.25)

g={\rm d}r^{2}+h(x,r)\,.

Moreover, the embedding
\Sigma\hookrightarrow(M,g)
is minimal. Here and above
x
denotes
some choice of coordinates along
\Sigma

which also give coordinates along hypersurfaces of constant
r
by pulling back the exponential map.
The boundary
\Sigma
is the hypersurface at
r=0
.
In
[
18
]
Fefferman and Graham established that asymptotic solutions to the Einstein condition for
{\mathring{g}}
exist such that

(5.26)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\textstyle h(x,r)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r\to 0}}{{\sim}}h^{\rm ev}+\sum_{\ell=d-1}h^{(\ell)}(x)r^{\ell}\,,&d{\rm\ even}\,,\\[8.53581pt]
h(x,r)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r\to 0}}{{\sim}}\ h^{\rm ev}+\beta(x)r^{d-1}\log r+h^{(d-1)}(x)r^{d-1}\,,&d{\rm\ odd}\,,\end{array}\right.

where

h^{\rm ev}:=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\big\lfloor\!\frac{d-2}{2}\!\big\rfloor}h^{(2\ell)}(x)r^{2\ell}.

In the above
h^{(0)}
gives the choice of boundary metric in
\boldsymbol{c}_{\Sigma}
. The absence of a term linear in
r
further implies that this embedding is totally geodesic. When
d
is even, the Dirichlet data
h^{(0)}
uniquely determines
the coefficients
h^{(1)},\ldots,h^{(d-2)}
, while higher coefficients are uniquely determined upon specifying suitable Neumann data
h^{(d-1)}
. Evenness of the expansion in
r
thus breaks at this order. Of course a global solution for
{\mathring{g}}
, when such is available, determines the quantity
h^{(d-1)}
. When
d
is odd,
the Dirichlet data
h^{(0)}
uniquely determines
the coefficients
h^{(1)},\ldots,h^{(d-2)}
. The coefficient
\beta(x)
of the log term gives the so-called Fefferman–Graham obstruction tensor
[
18
,
19
]
, and only depends on the conformal class of boundary metrics
\boldsymbol{c}_{\Sigma}
, while the Neumann data (undetermined by the asymptotic expansion) is again given by suitable
h^{(d-1)}
; see
[
31
]
.

Higher terms in the expansion for odd
d
and non-vanishing Fefferman–Graham obstruction tensor are of polylogarithmic type involving higher powers of
\log r
. The expansions above solve the Einstein condition asymptotically, in the sense

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\textstyle\mathring{P}^{{\mathring{g}}}(x,r)={\mathcal{O}}(r^{\infty})\,,&d{\rm\ even}\,,\\[8.53581pt]
\mathring{P}^{{\mathring{g}}}(x,r)={\mathcal{O}}(r^{d-2})+\,{\mathcal{O}}(\log r,r^{d-2})\,,&d{\rm\ odd}\,,\end{array}\right.

where
\mathring{P}^{{\mathring{g}}}
is the trace-free Schouten tensor of
{\mathring{g}}
and the notation
{\mathcal{O}}(\log r,r^{d-2})
denotes a polyhomogeneous expansion in
r
and
\log r
where all terms are at least order
r^{d-2}
.

There exist conformal fundamental forms
[
6
]
that measure the failure of a conformally compact structure to admit asymptotic solutions to the Einstein condition. These both capture the coefficients
h^{{(k)}}
determined by the
boundary Dirichlet data, as well as the leading Neumann data
[
7
]
(assuming special conditions on the boundary conformal class when
d
is odd).

5.2.
Scalar Poincaré–Einstein Boundary Operators

In Section
7
we construct the Yang–Mills boundary operators discussed in the introduction. Here we construct scalar boundary operators that exhibit salient properties of their Yang–Mills counterparts.
Firstly, the conformally invariant
Robin operator
[
10
]
, defined for any
conformally compact structure and given for some choice of scale by

\delta^{(1)}:\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\nabla_{\hat{n}}-wH\,,

maps bulk densities
\Gamma({\mathcal{E}}M[w])
to boundary densities
\Gamma({\mathcal{E}}\Sigma[w-1])
.
The above operator is a special case of Equation (
2.3
). It takes one normal derivative, so is said to have transverse order one. In general a smooth operator
\delta
mapping
\Gamma({\mathcal{E}}M[w])
to
\Gamma({\mathcal{E}}\Sigma[w^{\prime}])
is said to have
transverse order
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
if
\delta\circ\sigma^{k}\neq 0=\delta\circ\sigma^{k+1}
, where
\sigma
is a defining density for the boundary here viewed as a multiplication operator mapping
\Gamma({\mathcal{E}}M[w-1])
to
\Gamma({\mathcal{E}}M[w])
.
The general theory of
[
24
]
(see also
[
4
]
) leads in particular to transverse order two and three scalar boundary operators:

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
(M_{+},{\mathring{g}})
be a dimension
d
Poincaré–Einstein structure with conformal infinity
\Sigma
. Then the operators defined for any
g\in\boldsymbol{c}
by

\check{\delta}^{(2)}:\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}(d+2w-3)\Big[\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}-2(w-\tfrac{1}{2})H\nabla_{\hat{n}}+wP_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}+w(w-\tfrac{1}{2})H^{2}\Big]-(\bar{\Delta}+w\bar{J})

and

\check{\delta}^{(3)}:\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}(d+2w-5)\Big[\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}\hat{n}^{c}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\nabla_{c}-3(w-1)H\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\\
\qquad+(3w-1)P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}\nabla_{\hat{n}}+\tfrac{3}{2}(2w-1)(w-1)H^{2}\nabla_{\hat{n}}\hskip 8.5359pt\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+w\big((\nabla_{\hat{n}}J)+(\bar{\Delta}H)-(d+3w-4)P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}H-\tfrac{1}{2}(2w^{2}+d-3w)H^{3}+\bar{J}H\big)\\
-(\bar{\nabla}^{a}H)\bar{\nabla}_{a}\Big]\hskip 133.72786pt\\[2.84526pt]
\quad-3\big[\bar{\Delta}+(w-1)\bar{J}{\hskip 0.85358pt}\big]\delta^{(1)}\,,\hskip 270.30118pt

respectively map
\Gamma({\mathcal{E}}M[w])
to
\Gamma({\mathcal{E}}\Sigma[w-2])
and
\Gamma({\mathcal{E}}\Sigma[w-3])
.

When
d=3
, the operator

\delta^{(2_{3})}:\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}+H\nabla_{\hat{n}}

maps
C^{\infty}_{\ker\bar{\Delta}}M\to\Gamma({\mathcal{E}}\Sigma[-2])
where
C^{\infty}_{\ker\bar{\Delta}}M:=\big\{f\in C^{\infty}M\,\big|\,\bar{\Delta}(f|_{\Sigma})=0\big\}
.

Proof.

The operators
\check{\delta}^{(2)}
and
\check{\delta}^{(3)}
are both given, modulo lower order conformally invariant terms and in a more general context than here, in
[
24
]
.
The operator
\delta^{(2_{3})}
is new. It is a simple exercise to verify its claimed conformal variation property when acting on functions whose restriction to the boundary is harmonic.
∎

Remark 5.5
.

When
w
is
\frac{3-d}{2}
and
\frac{5-d}{2}
, respectively, the operators

\check{\delta}^{(2)}=-\bar{\square}\>\mbox{ and }\>\check{\delta}^{(3)}=-3{\hskip 0.85358pt}\bar{\square}\circ\delta^{(1)}\,,

where
\bar{\square}:=\bar{\Delta}+\frac{3-d}{2}\bar{J}

is the conformally invariant boundary Yamabe operator mapping
\Gamma({\mathcal{E}}\Sigma[\frac{3-d}{2}])\to\Gamma({\mathcal{E}}\Sigma[\frac{1-d}{2}])
.
Their respective transverse orders are then
0
and
1
rather than
2
and
3
in those cases.

\blacklozenge

The operators
\check{\delta}^{(1)}:=(d+2w-2)\delta^{(1)}
,
\check{\delta}^{(2)}
and
\check{\delta}^{(3)}
control the behaviour of (generalized) Laplacian eigenfunctions and harmonics. For the latter case consider the following harmonic problem for Poincaré–Einstein manifolds.

Problem 5.6
.

Let
(M_{+},g^{o})
be a dimension-
d
Poincaré–Einstein manifold and
B\in C^{\infty}\Sigma
be given. Find
f
, continuous on
M
and such that
f\in C^{\infty}M_{+}
, which obeys

\Delta^{g^{o}}f=0=f|_{\Sigma}-B\,.

\blacksquare

It is well known
[
41
,
42
,
35
]
that Problem
5.6

has a unique solution for
f\in C^{\infty}M_{+}
for
d=3,5,\ldots
and for
f\in C^{\infty}M
for
d=2,4,6,\ldots
. For
d
even there is a
canonical
Dirichlet-to-Neumann
map

{\mathscr{N}}:C^{\infty}\Sigma\to\Gamma({\mathcal{E}}\Sigma[1-d])\,,

whose input is the Dirchlet data
B
in Problem
5.6
.
The output is
defined by expanding the unique solution
f
in the canonical Fefferman–Graham coordinate
r
and extracting the coefficient
\frac{1}{(d-1)!}\frac{{\rm d}^{d-1}f}{{\rm d}r^{d-1}}\big|_{r=0}

of the odd order
r^{d-1}
term.
Evenness of the asymptotic expansion
of the solution
f
to order
r^{d-2}

can be used to show that this then
defines a weight
1-d
boundary density
[
35
]
.

The situation for odd dimensions
d
is more subtle. There, the unique, smooth global solution for
f
on
M_{+}
has a polyhomogeneous asymptotic series expansion
[
35
]

(5.27)

f(x,r)=B(x)+r^{2}f_{2}(x)+\cdots+r^{d-3}f_{d-3}(x)\\
+\tfrac{(-1)^{d}[(d-2))!!]^{2}}{(d-1)!(d-2)!}r^{d-1}\log r\,{\sf P}_{d-1}B(x)+{\mathscr{N}}_{\bar{h}}\big(B(x)\big)r^{d-1}+{\mathcal{O}}(r^{d})+\log r\,{\mathcal{O}}(r^{d})\,.

Here
\sf P_{d-1}
denotes a GJMS operator, namely a conformally invariant Laplacian power of the form
\bar{\Delta}^{{}^{\lfloor\frac{d-1}{2}\rfloor}}+{\rm LOTs}
where “
{\rm LOTs}
” denotes the lower derivative order terms required for conformal invariance
[
32
]
.
In the above expansion, the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map

{\mathscr{N}}_{\bar{h}}:C^{\infty}\Sigma\to C^{\infty}\Sigma\,,

defined by the coefficient of the
r^{d-1}
term, depends on the choice of boundary metric representative
\bar{h}\in\boldsymbol{c}_{\Sigma}

[
35
]
.
The boundary operators of Proposition
5.4
can be used to extract the image of the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map:

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
f
a solution to Problem
5.6

be given for a dimension
d
Poincaré–Einstein manifold with Dirichlet data
B\in C^{\infty}\Sigma
. Then, when

(i)

d\geq 3
,
\check{\delta}^{(1)}f=0
,

(ii)

d\geq 4
,
\check{\delta}^{(1)}f=\check{\delta}^{(2)}f=0
,

(iii)

d\geq 5
,
\check{\delta}^{(1)}f=\check{\delta}^{(2)}f=\check{\delta}^{(3)}f=0
.

Moreover, when
d=2,4
respectively,

\delta^{(1)}f={\mathscr{N}}(B)\>\mbox{ and }\>-\tfrac{1}{3!}\check{\delta}^{(3)}f={\mathscr{N}}(B)\,.

Also, when
d=3
and
\check{\delta}^{(2)}f=\bar{\Delta}B=0
, then
f\in C^{\infty}M
.
In this case the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map
{\mathscr{N}}_{\bar{h}}:C^{\infty}\Sigma\cap\ker\bar{\Delta}\to\Gamma({\mathcal{E}}\Sigma[-2])
and is given by the operator
\frac{1}{2!}\delta^{(2_{3})}
.

Proof.

First we establish that the operators
\check{\delta}^{(1)}
,
\check{\delta}^{(2)}
and
\check{\delta}^{(3)}
annihilate solutions in the claimed dimensions. An elegant proof approach relates these operators to
powers of the Laplace–Robin operator restricted to
\Sigma
, and then uses that the harmonic condition on functions is equivalent to lying in the kernel of that operator. Else, one may use that acting on
k\in C^{\infty}M_{+}
,
the singular metric Laplacian obeys

\sigma^{-1}\Delta^{g^{o}}k=\sigma\Delta k-(d-2)\nabla_{n}k\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}-(d-2)\delta^{(1)}k\,.

The last equality used that
n={\rm d}\sigma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\hat{n}
. Taking
k
to be a solution
f
, this establishes Point
(
i
)
, and in also that
\delta^{(1)}f=\nabla_{\hat{n}}f|_{\Sigma}=0

for
d\geq 3
.

Acting with
\nabla_{n}
on
\sigma^{-1}\Delta^{g^{o}}f
, applying Lemma
5.3
, and using that Equation (
5.27
) implies
f\in C^{d-2}M
for odd
d\geq 5
, gives

(5.28)

0=\sigma\nabla_{n}\Delta f+|n|^{2}\Delta f-(d-2)\nabla_{n}^{2}f\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\big(\bar{\Delta}-(d-3)\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}+H\nabla_{\hat{n}}\big)f=-\check{\delta}^{(2)}f+H\check{\delta}^{(1)}f\,.

Hence, when
d\geq 4
, we have
\check{\delta}^{(1)}f=\check{\delta}^{(2)}f=0
, as required for Point
(
ii
)
.

To prove Point
(
iii
)
,
we may use
\check{\delta}^{(1)}f=0=\check{\delta}^{(2)}f
so that

\nabla_{\hat{n}}f|_{\Sigma}=0\mbox{ and }\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}f|_{\Sigma}=\tfrac{1}{d-3}\bar{\Delta}f\,.

Hence

(5.29)

\check{\delta}^{(3)}f=(d-5)\Big[\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}\hat{n}^{c}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\nabla_{c}f+\tfrac{3}{d-3}H\bar{\Delta}f-(\bar{\nabla}^{a}H)\bar{\nabla}_{a}f\Big]\,.

To show that this vanishes for
d>5
we apply
\nabla_{n}
once again to Equation (
5.28
), giving

0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}2\nabla_{n}\Delta f+2H\Delta f-(d-2)\nabla_{n}^{3}f\,.

Here we used
\nabla_{n}|n|^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}2H
.
Now we develop each term above separately and find that

\displaystyle\nabla_{n}\Delta f

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\hat{n}^{a}(\bar{g}^{bc}+\hat{n}^{b}\hat{n}^{c})\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\nabla_{c}f

\displaystyle=\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}\hat{n}^{c}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\nabla_{c}f+\bar{g}^{bc}R_{\hat{n}bc}{}^{d}\nabla_{d}f+\hat{n}^{a}\bar{g}^{bc}\nabla^{\top}_{b}\nabla_{c}\nabla_{a}f

\displaystyle=\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}\hat{n}^{c}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\nabla_{c}f-Ric_{\hat{n}d}\bar{\nabla}^{d}f-H\nabla^{\top}_{a}\nabla^{a}f+\nabla_{b}^{\top}(\hat{n}^{a}\nabla^{b}\nabla_{a}f)

\displaystyle=\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}\hat{n}^{c}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\nabla_{c}f-(d-2)P_{\hat{n}a}\bar{\nabla}^{a}f-H\bar{\Delta}f-\bar{\nabla}_{a}(H\bar{\nabla}^{a}f)+\nabla^{\top}_{b}(\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla_{a}f)

\displaystyle=\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}\hat{n}^{c}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\nabla_{c}f-\tfrac{d-5}{d-3}H\bar{\Delta}f+(d-3)(\bar{\nabla}_{a}H)\bar{\nabla}^{a}f\,,

\displaystyle\Delta f\>\>

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\tfrac{d-2}{d-3}\bar{\Delta}f\,,

and

\displaystyle\nabla_{n}^{3}f

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\hat{n}^{b}\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla_{b}\nabla_{n}f+H\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla_{n}f

\displaystyle=\hat{n}^{b}\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla_{n}\nabla_{b}f+\hat{n}^{b}\nabla_{\hat{n}}\big((\nabla_{b}n^{c})\nabla_{c}f\big)+H\hat{n}^{a}\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla_{a}f\hskip 126.61467pt

\displaystyle=\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}\hat{n}^{c}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\nabla_{c}f+\tfrac{3}{d-3}H\bar{\Delta}f+(\bar{\nabla}_{a}H)\bar{\nabla}^{a}f\,.

For the second equality we used Equation (
5.3
) and the last line above relied on Equation (
5.16
).
Hence Equation (
5.28
) now implies

-(d-4)\Big[\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}\hat{n}^{c}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\nabla_{c}f+\tfrac{3}{d-3}H\bar{\Delta}f-(\bar{\nabla}_{a}H)\bar{\nabla}^{a}f\Big]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}0\,.

Having established Points
(
i
-
iii
)
, we turn to the Dirichlet–Neumann maps. For that we need to study the operators
\check{\delta}^{(k)}
(
k=1,2,3
)
in the Fefferman–Graham coordinate system. Note that the embedding
\Sigma\hookrightarrow(M,g)
for the compactified metric
g
of Equation (
5.26
) has zero mean curvature. Also, acting on scalars, the operator
\nabla_{n}=\frac{\partial}{\partial r}
.
That
\delta^{(1)}f={\mathscr{N}}(B)
when
d=2
now follows immediately.
When
d=4
, we now have

\check{\delta}^{(3)}f\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{=}}-\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}\hat{n}^{c}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\nabla_{c}f\,.

Using our above computation for
\nabla_{n}^{3}=\frac{\partial^{3}}{\partial r^{3}}
in terms of
\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}\hat{n}^{c}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\nabla_{c}f
, it readily follows that

-\tfrac{1}{3!}\check{\delta}^{(3)}f={\mathscr{N}}(B)
.

The smoothness statement for the
d=3
case was already established in
[
35
]
, so we must now consider the operator
\delta^{(2_{3})}
. The same considerations show that this operator now equals
\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial r^{2}}
and therefore extracts
2!
times the coefficient
{\mathscr{N}}_{\bar{h}}
of the
r^{2}
term in the expansion (
5.27
).
∎

Remark 5.8
.

When
d
is odd, solutions
f
to Problem
5.6
may not be smooth on
M
. For the
d=3
case, the obstruction to smoothness of solutions is
\check{\delta}^{(2)}f^{\rm ext}=-\bar{\Delta}B=-{\sf P}_{2}B
, where
f^{\rm ext}
is
any
smooth extension of the boundary Dirichlet data
B
to
M
.
Also, the vanishing results for the operators
\check{\delta}^{(k)}
on solutions can be used to extract
the coefficients in the expansion (
5.27
), in particular, in odd dimensions
d\geq 5
we have

f(x,r)=B(x)+\tfrac{1}{2!(d-3)}r^{2}\bar{\Delta}B(x)+{\mathcal{O}}(r^{4})+{\mathcal{O}}(r^{d-1}\log r)\,.

When
d=5
, the invariant operator
\check{\delta}^{(3)}=-3{\hskip 0.85358pt}\bar{\square}\circ\delta^{(1)}
does annihilate solutions to
Problem
5.6
, however it only has transverse order one. Instead, Equation (
5.29
)
suggests the operator

\delta^{(3_{5})}:=\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}\hat{n}^{c}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\nabla_{c}f+\tfrac{3}{2}H\bar{\Delta}f-(\bar{\nabla}^{a}H)\bar{\nabla}_{a}f\,.

In fact
\delta^{(3_{5})}
defines a
conformally invariant operator
acting on
C^{\infty}M\cap\ker\delta^{(1)}
that also annihilates solutions.

\blacklozenge

Non-linear analogs of the scalar boundary operators for the Poincaré–Einstein filling problem were given in
[
7
]
.
Non-linear
Yang–Mills analogs are given in Section (
7
).

5.3.
Poincaré–Einstein Asymptotically Yang–Mills Connections

We now provide some technical results concerning asymptotically Yang–Mills connections.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
(M^{d},\boldsymbol{c})
be an asymptotically Poincaré–Einstein structure with
d\geq 6
, equipped with an asymptotically Yang–Mills connection
\nabla
.
Then, for any
g\in\boldsymbol{c}
,

(5.30)

\nabla_{\hat{n}}F_{nb}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\frac{1}{d-5}\,\bar{\jmath}_{b}\,.

Also

(5.31)

\nabla_{\hat{n}}F_{ab}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\frac{2}{d-5}\,\hat{n}_{[a}{\bar{\jmath}}_{b]}-2H\bar{F}_{ab}

and

(5.32)

\nabla^{a}F_{ab}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\frac{d-4}{d-5}{\hskip 0.85358pt}{\hskip 0.85358pt}{\hskip 0.85358pt}\bar{\jmath}_{b}\,.

Proof.

Because
\nabla
is asymptotically Yang–Mills, we have
(d-4)F_{nb}=\sigma\nabla^{a}F_{ab}+{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma^{d-4})
, so acting on this relation with
\nabla_{n}
we obtain

(d-4)\nabla_{n}F_{nb}=\nabla_{n}(\sigma\nabla^{a}F_{ab})+{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma^{d-5})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\nabla^{\top}_{a}\bar{F}^{a}{}_{b}+\hat{n}^{a}\nabla_{\hat{n}}F_{ab}=\bar{\jmath}_{b}+\nabla_{\hat{n}}F_{nb}\,.

The second equality above used Equation (
5.15
) and that
F_{ab}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\bar{F}_{ab}
. The third equality relied on the tensor analog of Equation (
5.4
), Equation (
5.17
)
as well as
\bar{F}_{\hat{n}b}=0
. This yields Equation (
5.30
).

To obtain Equation (
5.31
) we employ the Bianchi identity to compute

(d-5)\nabla_{n}F_{ab}=-2(d-5)n^{c}\nabla_{[a}F_{b]c}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}-2(d-5)\nabla_{[a}F_{b]n}+2(d-5)HF_{ba}

=2\hat{n}_{[a}\bar{\jmath}_{b]}-2(d-5)H\bar{F}_{ab}\,.

Here we used Equation (
5.30
) as well as
\nabla_{a}n_{b}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}Hg_{ab}
and
\nabla_{[a}^{\top}F_{b]\hat{n}}^{\phantom{\top}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}0
.

Finally, Equation (
5.32
) follows from a simple computation

(d-5)\nabla^{a}F_{ab}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}(d-5)\nabla^{\top}_{a}F^{a}{}_{b}+(d-5)\hat{n}^{a}\nabla_{\hat{n}}F_{ab}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}(d-4)\bar{\jmath}_{b}\,,

where the last step used Equation (
5.31
).
∎

Identities involving two normal derivatives of the curvature of an asymptotically Yang–Mills connection are more involved and are recorded below.

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
(M^{d},\boldsymbol{c})
be an asymptotically Poincaré–Einstein structure with
d\geq 7
, equipped with an asymptotically Yang–Mills connection
\nabla
.
Then, for any
g\in\boldsymbol{c}
,

(5.33)

\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla^{a}F_{ab}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}-(d-4)\Big(P_{\hat{n}}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{cb}+\frac{2}{d-5}\,H\bar{\jmath}_{b}\Big)\,,

(5.34)

\hat{n}^{c}\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla_{c}F_{ab}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\frac{2}{d-5}{\hskip 0.85358pt}\bar{\nabla}_{[a}\bar{\jmath}_{b]}-2\bar{P}_{[a}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{b]c}+(2P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}+5H^{2})\bar{F}_{ab}+2\hat{n}_{[a}\Big(P_{\hat{n}}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{b]c}-\frac{5}{d-5}{\hskip 0.85358pt}H\bar{\jmath}_{b]}\Big)\,,

and

(5.35)

\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla_{a}F_{bc}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}P_{\hat{n}}{}^{a}\bar{F}_{ca}-\frac{5}{d-5}{\hskip 0.85358pt}H\bar{\jmath}_{c}\,.

Proof.

To obtain Equation (
5.33
) we compute as follows

\displaystyle\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla^{a}F_{ab}\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\

\displaystyle\nabla^{a}\nabla_{n}F_{ab}-Ric_{\hat{n}}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{cb}+R_{\hat{n}}{}^{a}{}_{b}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{ac}-(\nabla^{a}n^{c})\nabla_{c}F_{ab}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\

\displaystyle-2\nabla^{a}(n^{c}\nabla_{[a}F_{b]c})-(d-3)P_{\hat{n}}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{cb}-H\nabla^{a}F_{ab}\,.

The second equality used Equations (
5.5
) and (
5.16
) as well as
\hat{n}^{a}\bar{F}_{ab}=0
.
Thus, because
n^{c}\nabla_{[a}F_{b]c}=\nabla_{[a}F_{b]n}-(\nabla_{[a}n^{c})F_{b]c}
and
(d-4)F_{an}=\sigma\nabla^{b}F_{ba}+{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma^{d-4})
, we have in dimensions
d\geq 7
that

\displaystyle(d-4)(\nabla_{\hat{n}}+H)\nabla^{a}F_{ab}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\

\displaystyle-2\nabla^{a}\nabla_{[a}(\sigma\nabla^{c}F_{b]c})

\displaystyle+(d-4)\Big(2(\nabla^{a}\nabla_{[a}n^{c})F_{b]c}+2H\nabla^{a}F_{ba}-(d-3)P_{\hat{n}}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{cb}\Big)\,.

Thus

\displaystyle(d-4)(\nabla_{\hat{n}}+3H)\nabla^{a}F_{ab}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\

\displaystyle-2\hat{n}^{a}\nabla_{[a}\nabla^{c}F_{b]c}-2\nabla^{a}(n_{[a}\nabla^{c}F_{b]c})

\displaystyle+(d-4)\Big(-2\big(\hat{n}^{a}P_{[a}{}^{c}+\delta_{[a}^{c}P_{\hat{n}}{}^{a}\big)\bar{F}_{b]c}-(d-3)P_{\hat{n}}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{cb}\Big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\

\displaystyle 2\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla^{a}F_{ab}+\hat{n}^{a}\nabla_{b}\nabla^{c}F_{ac}+\hat{n}_{b}\nabla^{a}\nabla^{c}F_{ac}-2H\delta^{a}_{[a}\nabla^{c}F_{b]c}

\displaystyle-(d-4)(d-6)P_{\hat{n}}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{cb}\,.

Here the first equality relied on Equation (
5.21
) while the second employed Equation (
5.16
).
Thence, using
2\nabla^{a}\nabla^{c}F_{ac}=[F^{ac},F_{ac}]=0
, we have

(d-6)\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla^{a}F_{ab}+{\hskip 0.85358pt}\hat{n}_{b}\hat{n}^{a}\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla^{c}F_{ca}+(2d-11)H\nabla^{a}F_{ab}\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\ \hat{n}^{a}\nabla^{\top}_{b}\nabla^{c}F_{ac}-(d-4)(d-6)P_{\hat{n}}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{cb}\,.

But

(d-4){\hskip 0.85358pt}\hat{n}^{b}\nabla^{a}F_{ab}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\nabla^{a}(\sigma\nabla^{c}F_{ac})

so
(d-3)\hat{n}^{b}\nabla^{a}F_{ab}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}0
, whence contracting the above display with
\hat{n}^{b}
we find

\hat{n}^{a}\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla^{c}F_{ca}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}0
.
Thus we learn that

\displaystyle(d-5)(d-6)\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla^{a}F_{ab}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\ (d-4)\big(-\hat{n}^{a}\nabla_{b}^{\top}{\bar{\jmath}}_{a}^{\rm ext}-(2d-11)H\bar{\jmath}_{b}-(d-5)(d-6)P_{\hat{n}}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{cb}\big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}-(d-4)(d-6)\big(2H\bar{\jmath}_{b}+(d-5)P_{\hat{n}}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{cb}\big)\,.

In first equality above we used Equation (
5.32
). The second used that
\nabla^{\top}_{a}\hat{n}_{b}^{\rm ext}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\bar{g}_{ab}H
. Since
d\geq 7
, the claimed result holds.

To establish Equation (
5.36
) we compute that

\displaystyle\nabla_{n}^{2}F_{ab}\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\

\displaystyle-2\nabla_{\hat{n}}(n^{c}\nabla_{[a}F_{b]c})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\

\displaystyle-2\nabla_{\hat{n}}\big(\nabla_{[a}F_{b]n}-(\nabla_{[a}n^{c})F_{b]c}\big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{d-4}\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla_{[a}(\sigma\nabla^{c}F_{b]c})+2(\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla_{[a}n^{c})\bar{F}_{b]c}+2H\nabla_{\hat{n}}F_{ba}\,.

The first equality above relies on the Bianchi identity for the curvature
F
while the third employed the asymptotically Yang–Mills condition in dimensions
d\geq 7
as well as Equation (
5.16
).
Hence

\displaystyle\nabla_{n}^{2}F_{ab}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{d-4}\big(\nabla_{[a}\nabla^{c}F_{b]c}+\nabla_{\hat{n}}(n_{[a}\nabla^{c}F_{b]c})\big)

\displaystyle-2(P_{[a}{}^{c}+P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}\delta_{[a}^{c})\bar{F}_{b]c}-2H\big(\frac{2}{d-5}\hat{n}_{[a}\bar{\jmath}_{b]}-2H\bar{F}_{ab}\big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\

\displaystyle\frac{2}{d-5}(\nabla^{\top}_{[a}-2H\hat{n}_{[a})\bar{\jmath}_{b]}-\frac{2}{d-4}\hat{n}_{[a}(2\nabla_{\hat{n}}+H)\nabla^{c}F_{b]c}

\displaystyle-2\big(P_{[a}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{b]c}-P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}\bar{F}_{ab}\big)+4H^{2}\bar{F}_{ab}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\

\displaystyle\frac{2}{d-5}\bar{\nabla}_{[a}\bar{\jmath}_{b]}-4\hat{n}_{[a}\Big(P_{\hat{n}}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{|c|b]}+\frac{2}{d-5}\,H\bar{\jmath}_{b]}\Big)

\displaystyle-2\big(P_{[a}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{b]c}-P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}\bar{F}_{ab}\big)+4H^{2}\bar{F}_{ab}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\

\displaystyle\frac{2}{d-5}\Big(\bar{\nabla}_{[a}\!-4{\hskip 0.85358pt}H\hat{n}_{[a}\Big)\bar{\jmath}_{b]}-2\big(\bar{P}_{[a}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{b]c}-\hat{n}_{[a}P_{\hat{n}}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{b]c}-P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}\bar{F}_{ab}\big)+3H^{2}\bar{F}_{ab}\,.

The first equality above used Equations (
5.20
) and (
5.31
). The second equality used Equation (
5.32
). The third equality used Equations (
5.4
), (
5.32
) and (
5.33
).
The last equality relied on Equation (
5.7
). Rearranging the above we have

(5.36)

\nabla_{n}^{2}F_{ab}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\frac{2}{d-5}{\hskip 0.85358pt}\bar{\nabla}_{[a}\bar{\jmath}_{b]}-2\bar{P}_{[a}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{b]c}+(2P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}+3H^{2})\bar{F}_{ab}+2\hat{n}_{[a}\Big(P_{\hat{n}}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{b]c}-\frac{4}{d-5}{\hskip 0.85358pt}H\bar{\jmath}_{b]}\Big)\,.

An application of Equations (
5.17
) and (
5.31
)
to the left hand side above yields the quoted result. Finally, Equation (
5.35
) follows directly from Equation (
5.34
) upon contraction with a unit normal vector.

∎

6.
Renormalization, Anomalies and Energies

Integrals over conformally compact manifolds
run the risk of being ill-defined since the metric is singular along the boundary.
Nonetheless, useful information can often be
extracted from the asymptotics of integrals over families of regions suitably approaching the boundary:
Let
(M,\boldsymbol{c},\sigma)
be conformally compact with singular metric

{\mathring{g}}=\frac{\mbox{\boldmath{$g$}}}{\sigma^{2}}\,,

on its interior
M_{+}
. We wish to construct a one-parameter
\varepsilon>0
family of “regulating manifolds”
M_{\varepsilon}
,
for each of which
(M_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{c},\sigma)
is a Riemannian structure with non-singular metric
{\mathring{g}}
.
For that, given any true scale
0<\tau\in\Gamma({\mathcal{E}}M[1])
, we define

(6.1)

M_{\varepsilon}:=\{p\in M\,|\,\sigma(p)/\tau(p)>\varepsilon\}\subset M_{+}\,.

Observe that
0\leq\sigma/\tau\in C^{\infty}M
and
\Sigma=\partial M
is the zero locus of the smooth defining function
\sigma/\tau
.
Moreover
\sigma-\tau\varepsilon
is a defining density for
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}=\partial M_{\varepsilon}
.
Note that
in the zero
\varepsilon
limit we recover
M_{+}
from
M_{\varepsilon}
. Also, the restriction
\bar{\tau}:=\tau|_{\Sigma}
to
\Sigma
of
the density
\tau
determines a boundary true scale, and in turn a metric
{\bar{g}}={\bar{\mbox{\boldmath$g$}}}/\bar{\tau}\in\boldsymbol{c}_{\Sigma}
, where
\bar{\mbox{\boldmath{$g$}}}
is the conformal metric on
\Sigma
.
We call the data
\tau
a
choice of regulator
.

We are primarily interested in the asymptotics of the Yang–Mills functional on
M
.
Thus we define the
regulated Yang–Mills action functional
determined by a true scale
\tau
,

(6.2)

S^{\varepsilon}[A]:=-\frac{1}{4}\int_{M^{\varepsilon}}{\rm d}{\rm Vol}({\mathring{g}})\,{\rm Tr}({\mathring{g}}^{ab}{\mathring{g}}^{cd}F_{ac}F_{bd})\,.

Lemma 6.1
.

The regulated Yang–Mills functional is extremal with respect to
compactly supported variations of
A
when
the conformally compact Yang–Mills current satisfies

j_{a}[A]=0\,.

Proof.

First note that

S^{\varepsilon}[A]=-\frac{1}{4}\int_{M^{\varepsilon}}\frac{{\rm d}{\rm Vol}(\mbox{\boldmath$g$})}{\sigma^{d-4}}\,\operatorname{Tr}(F^{ab}F_{ab})\,,

where indices are raised and lowered with the conformal metric
g
.
Let
A_{t}
be a one-parameter family of gauge connections on
M
such that
A_{t}=A
everywhere outside a compactly supported region and such that
A_{0}=A
. Also define the endomorphism-valued one-form

\delta A:=\frac{{\rm d}A_{t}}{{\rm d}t}\Big|_{t=0}\,.

Then

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}S^{\varepsilon}[A_{t}]}{{\rm d}t}\Big|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{M^{\varepsilon}}\frac{{\rm d}{\rm Vol}(\mbox{\boldmath$g$})}{\sigma^{d-4}}\,\operatorname{Tr}(F^{ab}\delta F_{ab})

\displaystyle=\int_{M^{\varepsilon}}{\rm d}{\rm Vol}(\mbox{\boldmath$g$})\,\operatorname{Tr}\big(\nabla_{a}(\sigma^{4-d}F^{ab})\delta A_{b}\big)\,.

Here the operator
\delta
denotes

\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}{\hskip 0.85358pt}{\hskip 0.85358pt}{\boldsymbol{\hbox to2.5pt{\hss$\cdot$\hss}}}{\hskip 0.85358pt}{\hskip 0.85358pt}\big|_{t=0}
. Note that
\delta F_{ab}=2\nabla_{[a}\delta A_{b]}
. The compact support assumption was used in the second line.
The proof is completed by verifying that

(6.3)

\nabla_{a}(\sigma^{4-d}F^{ab})=\sigma^{3-d}j^{b}\,.

∎

We now prove Theorem
1.6
giving the asymptotics of
S^{\varepsilon}[A]
of Equation (
6.2
).

Proof of Theorem
1.6
.

The proof employs the methods of
[
27
]
.
To begin with, the regulated Yang–Mills action functional can be expressed in terms of an integral over
M
whose integral is weighted by a Heaviside step function

(6.4)

S^{\varepsilon}[A]=-\frac{1}{4}\int_{\tilde{M}}\frac{{\rm d}{\rm Vol}(\mbox{\boldmath$g$})}{\sigma^{d-4}}\,\theta\Big(\frac{\sigma}{\tau}-\varepsilon\Big)\,\operatorname{Tr}F^{2}\,.

In the above,
\tilde{M}
denotes an extension of
M
beyond
\partial M=\Sigma
by any collar neighborhood. This does not change the above integral since its support is on
M^{\varepsilon}
.
This technical maneuver is necessary for the following distribution-based computations, and is described in detail in
[
27
]
.

Using the distributional identity

\theta^{\prime}(x)=\delta(x)
, we now study

\displaystyle-\varepsilon^{d-4}\frac{{\rm d}S^{\varepsilon}[A]}{{\rm d}\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\varepsilon^{d-4}}{4}\!\!\int_{\tilde{M}}\frac{{\rm d}{\rm Vol}(\mbox{\boldmath$g$})}{\sigma^{d-4}}\,\delta\Big(\frac{\sigma}{\tau}-\varepsilon\Big)\operatorname{Tr}F^{2}

\displaystyle=\>\>\;-\frac{1}{4}\int_{\tilde{M}}\frac{{\rm d}{\rm Vol}(\mbox{\boldmath$g$})}{\tau^{d-5}}\,\delta\big({\sigma}-{\tau}\varepsilon\big)\operatorname{Tr}F^{2}\,.

To obtain the last equality recall that, for any test function
f
, under an integral
f(x)\delta(x)=f(0)\delta(x)
, and also
\delta(x/f(x))=f(x)\delta(x)
so long as
f(x)>0
. Also note this implies that a quantity such as
\delta(\sigma)
is a distribution-valued density of weight
-1
.

The right hand side of the above display is a regular function of
\varepsilon
around
\varepsilon=0
, so we expand it as a Taylor series in
\varepsilon
to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\tilde{M}}\frac{{\rm d}{\rm Vol}(\mbox{\boldmath$g$})}{\tau^{d-5}}\,\delta\big({\sigma}-{\tau}\varepsilon\big)\,\operatorname{Tr}F^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\tilde{M}}\frac{{\rm d}{\rm Vol}(\mbox{\boldmath$g$})}{\tau^{d-5}}\,\delta\big({\sigma}\big)\,\operatorname{Tr}F^{2}

\displaystyle+\varepsilon\int_{\tilde{M}}\frac{{\rm d}{\rm Vol}(\mbox{\boldmath$g$})}{\tau^{d-6}}\,\delta^{\prime}\big({\sigma}\big)\,\operatorname{Tr}F^{2}+\cdots

\displaystyle+\frac{\varepsilon^{d-5}}{(d-5)!}\int_{\tilde{M}}{\rm d}{\rm Vol}(\mbox{\boldmath$g$})\,\delta^{(d-5)}\big({\sigma}\big)\,\operatorname{Tr}F^{2}+{\mathcal{O}}\big(\varepsilon^{d-4}\big)\,.

The following identity for differentiated delta functions of a defining density is proved in
[
27
]
,

\Big(\frac{1}{I^{2}}{\hskip 0.85358pt}I.D\Big)^{\ell}\delta(\sigma)=(d-\ell-1)\cdots(d-3)(d-2)\delta^{(\ell)}(\sigma)\,,\qquad\ell\leq d-1\,.

In the same reference, it is shown that the Laplace–Robin operator
is formally self-adjoint.
Moreover, if
f
is any weight
-d+1
density, then

(6.5)

\int_{\tilde{M}}{\rm d}{\rm Vol}(\mbox{\boldmath$g$})\delta(\sigma)\sqrt{I^{2}}{\hskip 0.85358pt}f=\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}{\rm Vol}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bar{g}$})f\,.

Orchestrating the above observations,
it follows that

\int_{\tilde{M}}\frac{{\rm d}{F^{A}\rm Vol}(\mbox{\boldmath$g$})}{\tau^{d-5}}\,\delta\big({\sigma}-{\tau}\varepsilon\big)\,\operatorname{Tr}F^{2}=\\
\int_{\Sigma}\frac{{\rm d}{\rm Vol}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bar{g}$})}{\sqrt{I^{2}}{\hskip 0.85358pt}\tau^{d-5}}\,\operatorname{Tr}F^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{d-2}\int_{\Sigma}\frac{{\rm d}{\rm Vol}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bar{g}$})}{\sqrt{I^{2}}}\,I.D\Big(\frac{\operatorname{Tr}F^{2}}{I^{2}{\hskip 0.85358pt}\tau^{d-6}}\Big)+\cdots\qquad\qquad\\
+\frac{3!\,\varepsilon^{d-5}}{(d-5)!(d-2)!}\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}{\rm Vol}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bar{g}$}){\sqrt{I^{2}}}\,{\hskip 0.85358pt}\Big(\frac{1}{I^{2}}I.D\Big)^{d-5}\,\Big(\frac{\operatorname{Tr}F^{2}}{I^{2}}\Big)+{\mathcal{O}}\big(\varepsilon^{d-4}\big)\,.

Notice that the last-displayed integral does not depend on the choice of regulator
\tau
and hence is an invariant of the underlying structure. Dividing the above display by
-\varepsilon^{d-4}
and then integrating over
\varepsilon
gives the quoted result.
∎

In quantum field theory vernacular, the coefficients
v_{\ell}/\ell
of the
1/\varepsilon^{\ell}
poles above are termed
divergences
. Theorem
1.6
establishes that the divergences of the regulated Yang–Mills functional are boundary integrals over local quantities. They are however dependent on the
choice of regulator

{\tau}
.
The
\varepsilon
independent contribution
S^{\rm ren}
is termed the
renormalized action
and is not expressed as a
local boundary integral. Instead it probes global aspects of the system. Theorems
1.7
and
1.8
give its dependence on the choice of regulator. The proofs are as follows:

Proof of Theorem
1.7
.

We begin by considering the regulated Yang–Mills functional for the choice of regulator
e^{t\omega}\tau
where
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\omega\in C^{\infty}M
. Using Equation (
6.4
), this is given by

S^{\varepsilon}[A;e^{t\omega}\tau]=-\frac{1}{4}\int_{\tilde{M}}\frac{{\rm d}{\rm Vol}(\mbox{\boldmath$g$})}{\sigma^{d-4}}\,\theta\Big(\frac{\sigma}{\tau}-e^{-t\omega}\varepsilon\Big)\,\operatorname{Tr}F^{2}\,.

Differentiating with respect to
t
and recycling the
computation used to prove Theorem
1.6
gives

\frac{{\rm d}S^{\varepsilon}[A;e^{t\omega}\tau]}{{\rm d}t}=-\frac{1}{4{\hskip 0.85358pt}\varepsilon^{d-5}}{\hskip 0.85358pt}\int_{\tilde{M}}\frac{{\rm d}{\rm Vol}(\mbox{\boldmath$g$})}{\tau^{d-4}}\,\delta\Big(\frac{\sigma}{\tau}-e^{-t\omega}\varepsilon\Big)\,\omega e^{(d-5)t\omega}\operatorname{Tr}F^{2}\,.

The above can be expressed as a Laurent series in
\varepsilon
. We need to compute the coefficient of
\varepsilon^{0}
to study how
S^{\rm ren}[A]
responds to changes of the regulator.
This is given by

\frac{1}{(d-5)!}{\hskip 0.85358pt}\int_{\tilde{M}}{\rm d}{\rm Vol}(\mbox{\boldmath$g$})\,\delta^{(d-5)}\big({\sigma}\big)\,\omega{\hskip 0.85358pt}\operatorname{Tr}F^{2}\\
=\frac{3!}{(d-5)!(d-2)!}\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}{\rm Vol}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bar{g}$})\,{\sqrt{I^{2}}}\,{\hskip 0.85358pt}\Big(\frac{1}{I^{2}}I.D\Big)^{d-5}\,\Big(\frac{\operatorname{Tr}F^{2}}{I^{2}}{\hskip 0.85358pt}\omega\Big)\,.

The result follows upon observing that this is independent of
t
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.8
.

This is an direct consequence of the evenness property of the Fefferman–Graham type expansions developed in Section
7.1
; further details are discussed there.
∎

Remark 6.2
.

Theorem
1.7
can also be easily extended to dimension
d=5
when the expansion in Theorem
1.6
has no negative powers of
\varepsilon
and instead
\log\varepsilon
is its leading term.
Specializing
Theorem
1.7

to constant
\omega=\log\Lambda
(say) one has

S^{\rm ren}[A;\Lambda\tau]-S^{\rm ren}[A;\tau]=\Lambda\,{\rm En}[A]\,.

Hence, the energy functional
{\rm En}[A]
is termed an
anomaly
since it encodes the anomalous response of the renormalized action to
constant rescalings of the metric
g_{\scalebox{.7}{$\tau$}}:={\mbox{\boldmath$g$}}/{\tau^{2}}\in\boldsymbol{c}
determined by the regulator
\tau
.

\blacklozenge

Another key property of the energy
{\rm En}[A]

is the relation between its functional gradient and
the obstruction to smoothly solving the Yang–Mills equations on the conformally compact structure
(M,\boldsymbol{c},\sigma)
given in Theorem
1.10
, whose proof is as follows:

Proof of Theorem
1.10
.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
A[\bar{A}_{t}]
has compact support in the extended manifold
\tilde{M}
(as defined in the proof of Theorem
1.6
) because
A[{\bar{A}_{t}}]
is, in any case, determined only asymptotically. Also, since Theorem
4.1
shows that any differing asymptotic Yang-Mills connections are related by a gauge transformation (at least to sufficiently high order), the energy
\operatorname{En}[A[\bar{A}_{t}]]
is independent of this difference.

Now,
from Theorem
1.6
, the coefficient of
\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
in
{\rm d}S^{\varepsilon}\big[A[\bar{A}_{t}]\big]/{\rm d}t\big|_{t=0}

equals the variation of the energy
{\rm En}\big[A[{\bar{A}}]\big]
.
Let us denote
\delta A:={\rm d}A\big[{\bar{A}}]/{\rm d}t\big|_{t=0}
. Hence, starting from Equation (
6.4
), we compute

\displaystyle{\rm d}S^{\varepsilon}\big[A[\bar{A}_{t}]\big]/{\rm d}t\big|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\tilde{M}}\frac{{\rm d}{\rm Vol}(\mbox{\boldmath$g$})}{\sigma^{d-4}}\,\theta\Big(\frac{\sigma}{\tau}-\varepsilon\Big)\,\operatorname{Tr}(F^{ab}\,\nabla_{a}\delta A_{b})

\displaystyle=\int_{\tilde{M}}\frac{{\rm d}{\rm Vol}(\mbox{\boldmath$g$})}{\sigma}\,\theta\Big(\frac{\sigma}{\tau}-\varepsilon\Big)\,\operatorname{Tr}(k^{b}\,\delta A_{b})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{d-4}}\int_{\tilde{M}}\frac{{\rm d}{\rm Vol}(\mbox{\boldmath$g$})}{\tau^{d-5}}\,\delta\big({\sigma}-{\tau}\varepsilon\big)\,\operatorname{Tr}\Big(F^{ab}\,\Big(\nabla_{a}{\hskip 0.85358pt}\frac{\sigma}{\tau}\Big){\hskip 0.85358pt}\delta A_{b}\Big)\,,

where again indices are raised and lowered with
the conformal metric.
Also
\nabla
is the connection of
A[{\bar{A}}]
. In the second line we integrated by parts discarding boundary terms thanks to the compact support of
A[{\bar{A}}_{t}]
, and then used Equations (
6.3
,
1.6
)
plus the distributional calculus identities explained earlier.

The second term of the above display is easily seen to be regular in
\varepsilon
when multiplied by
\varepsilon^{d-4}
, and therefore cannot produce a term proportional to
\log\varepsilon
. The small
\varepsilon
asymptotics of first term on the right hand side of the above display can be determined
by exactly the same methods used to establish
Theorem
1.6
. Its
\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
coefficient can be computed
from that of the
-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
contribution to the first
\varepsilon
derivative of this term. This gives

-\int_{\tilde{M}}{{\rm d}{\rm Vol}(\mbox{\boldmath$g$})}\,\delta\big({\sigma}\big)\,\operatorname{Tr}(k^{a}\,\delta A_{a})=-\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}{\rm Vol}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bar{g}$})\,\frac{\operatorname{Tr}(k_{a}\,{\hskip 0.85358pt}\delta A^{a}_{\Sigma})}{\sqrt{I^{2}}}

as the coefficient of the
\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
term. For the last equality above we employed
Equation (
6.5
) and the fact that

\delta{\bar{A}}:=\frac{{\rm d}{\bar{A}}}{{\rm d}t}\Big|_{t=0}=\frac{{\rm d}A\big[{\bar{A}}]}{{\rm d}t}\Big|_{t=0,\Sigma}\,.

This used that an asymptotically Yang–Mills connection obeys
A[{\bar{A}}]={\bar{A}}^{\rm ext}+{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma)
, where
{\bar{A}}^{\rm ext}
is any smooth extension of
{\bar{A}}
to
M
.
The display before last establishes that the obstruction current
\bar{k}=k|_{\Sigma}
is proportional to the functional gradient of the energy
{\rm En}(A[{\bar{A}}])
.
∎

Remark 6.3
.

It is also possible to directly verify that the energy
{\rm En}[A]
is extremal with respect to arbitrary compactly supported variations of
A
whenever the Yang–Mills current
{j}[A]
obeys

{j}[A]={\mathcal{O}}(\sigma^{d-3})\,.

To see this, note that from Theorem
1.6
, in dimensions
d\geq 5
the holographic energy formula in Equation (
1.10
)
can equivalently be written as

{\rm En}[A]=-\frac{1}{4(d-5)!}\int_{\tilde{M}}{{\rm d}{\rm Vol}(\mbox{\boldmath$g$})}\delta^{(d-5)}(\sigma)\operatorname{Tr}F^{2}\,.

Thus, because
\nabla_{a}\delta^{(k)}(\sigma)=n_{a}\delta^{(k+1)}(\sigma)
, it follows that

\frac{{\rm d}{\rm En}\big[A_{t}\big]}{{\rm d}t}\Big|_{t=0}=-\int_{\tilde{M}}{{\rm d}{\rm Vol}(\mbox{\boldmath$g$})}\operatorname{Tr}\left(\Big[\delta^{(d-4)}(\sigma)\,n_{a}F^{ab}+\delta^{(d-5)}(\sigma){\hskip 0.85358pt}\nabla_{a}F^{ab}\Big]\delta A_{b}\right)\,.

The identity
\delta^{(d-5)}(\sigma)=-\frac{1}{d-4}\sigma{\hskip 0.85358pt}\delta^{(d-4)}(\sigma)
then gives

\frac{{\rm d}{\rm En}\big[A_{t}\big]}{{\rm d}t}\Big|_{t=0}=\frac{1}{d-4}\int_{\tilde{M}}{{\rm d}{\rm Vol}(\mbox{\boldmath$g$})}\delta^{(d-4)}(\sigma)\,\operatorname{Tr}\big({j}^{a}\delta A_{a}\big)\,.

The claim follows by noting, for any positive integer
\ell
, that
{\sigma}^{\ell+1}\delta^{(\ell)}({\sigma})=0
.

\blacklozenge

6.1.
The Energy

Recursions that compute both the energy
\operatorname{En}[A[\bar{A}]]=:\overline{\operatorname{En}}[\bar{A}]
and the obstruction
can be constructed using only knowledge of
the existence of asymptotically Yang–Mills connections
A[\bar{A}]
determined to sufficient order by
\bar{A}
.
The latter property was established in
Theorem
1.2
.
Here we exhibit such recursions in dimensions
d=5,6,7
.
There is no in-principle obstacle to performing these computations for any conformally compact structure, but for reasons of both simplicity and maximal relevance we focus on Poincaré–Einstein structures.

6.1.1.
The Energy for
d=5,6,7

The holographic formula in Equation (
1.10
) and identities of Section (
5
) allow us to compute the energy for low dimensional Poincaré–Einstein structures.

Proof of Theorem
1.9
.

Theorems
1.2
and
4.1
establish that a boundary connection
\bar{A}
uniquely determines an asymptotically Yang–Mills connection
A[\bar{A}]
to order
{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma^{d-3})
. However it is not difficult, upon employing Lemma
2.3
, to check that
\Big(\frac{1}{I^{2}}I.D\Big)^{d-5}\Big(\frac{-\operatorname{Tr}F^{2}}{4I^{2}}\Big)
only depends on the connection
A
to order
{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma^{d-4})
. Gauge invariance of the holographic energy formula (
1.10
) shows that
{\operatorname{En}}[A]
is completely determined by the boundary data
\bar{A}
.

In the remainder of the proof we rely heavily on the fact that an asymptotically Einstein structure
dimension obeys

\nabla_{a}n_{b}+\sigma P_{ab}+\rho g_{ab}={\mathcal{O}}(\sigma^{d-2})
and
I^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{=}}|n|_{g}^{2}+2\rho\sigma=1+{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma^{d-1})
.
Consulting Equation (
1.10
), we see that when
d=5

we have

Q[{\bf g},A]=-\tfrac{1}{4}\operatorname{Tr}F^{2}|_{\Sigma}=-\tfrac{1}{4}\operatorname{Tr}{\bar{F}}^{2}\,.

For the case
d=6
the same equation tells us to compute

\frac{1}{4}{\hskip 0.85358pt}I.D\operatorname{Tr}(F_{ab}F^{ab})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}-(\nabla_{\hat{n}}+4H)\operatorname{Tr}(F_{ab}F^{ab})=\operatorname{Tr}\big(-2\bar{F}^{ab}\nabla_{\hat{n}}F_{ab}-4H{\bar{F}}^{2}\big)=0\,.

In the last step we used Lemma
5.31
and
F_{\hat{n}a}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}0
, as well as the definition of the
I.D
operator acting on the weight
-4
density
\operatorname{Tr}(F_{ab}F^{ab})
along
\Sigma
; see Equation (
2.3
).

It remains to handle the case
d=7
for which,
again consulting Equation (
1.10
), we see that
we must compute
\frac{1}{40}I.D^{2}\operatorname{Tr}F^{2}
.
Notice that because
\operatorname{Tr}F^{2}
is a density of weight
-4
, from Equation (
2.1
) we have (remembering
\nabla_{n}\sigma=|n|^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}1
)

I.D^{2}\operatorname{Tr}F^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}(d-12)(\nabla_{n}-5\rho)\big[(d-10)(\nabla_{n}-4\rho)-\sigma(\Delta-4J)\big]\operatorname{Tr}F^{2}\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d=7}}{{=}}5\Big[\Delta-4J+3(\nabla_{n}-5\rho)(\nabla_{n}-4\rho)\Big]\operatorname{Tr}F^{2}\,.

Using
\rho\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}-H
and
\nabla_{n}\rho\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}
(see Lemma
5.2
and its proof) we have

\frac{1}{5}I.D^{2}\operatorname{Tr}F^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\Big[\Delta+3\nabla_{n}^{2}+27H\nabla_{n}-12P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}+60H^{2}-4J\Big]\operatorname{Tr}F^{2}\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\Big[\bar{\Delta}+4\nabla_{n}^{2}+32H\nabla_{n}-12P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}+60H^{2}-4J\Big]\operatorname{Tr}F^{2}\,.

In the above we used Lemma
5.3

and the identity
\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\nabla_{n}^{2}-H\nabla_{n}
to relate interior and boundary Laplacians
according to

\Delta f\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\bar{\Delta}\bar{f}+\nabla_{n}^{2}f+5H\nabla_{n}f
.

Now, from Equation
5.31
and
F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}{\bar{F}}
, it follows that

F^{ab}\nabla_{n}F_{ab}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}-2H{\bar{F}}^{2}\,.

Similarly

(\nabla_{n}F^{ab})\nabla_{n}F_{ab}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\frac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.85358pt}{\bar{\jmath}}^{2}+4H^{2}{\bar{F}}^{2}\,.

In addition note that

(\nabla_{n}F_{ab})^{\top}=-2HF_{ab}^{\Sigma}
,
and that (see Lemma
5.33
)

\displaystyle(\nabla_{n}^{2}F_{ab})^{\top}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\bar{\nabla}_{[a}{\bar{\jmath}}_{b]}-2\bar{P}_{[a}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{b]c}+2P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}\bar{F}_{ab}+3H^{2}\bar{F}_{ab}\,.

Also Lemma
5.9
tells us that

\nabla^{a}F_{ab}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\frac{3}{2}{\hskip 0.85358pt}\bar{\jmath}_{b}
.

Orchestrating the above identities (using the Ricci identity written in
d=7
in terms of the Schouten tensor as in Equation (
5.7
) and
J=\bar{J}+P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}-3H^{2}
from
Equation (
5.6
)), we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{5}I.D^{2}\operatorname{Tr}F^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\big(\bar{\Delta}-16P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}+72H^{2}-4\bar{J}\big)\operatorname{Tr}{\bar{F}}^{2}+4\nabla_{n}^{2}\operatorname{Tr}F^{2}+32H\nabla_{n}\operatorname{Tr}F^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\big(\bar{\Delta}-4\bar{J}\big)\operatorname{Tr}{\bar{F}}^{2}+4\operatorname{Tr}{\bar{\jmath}}^{2}+8\operatorname{Tr}({\bar{F}}^{ab}\bar{\nabla}_{[a}{\bar{\jmath}}_{b]})-16\operatorname{Tr}({\bar{F}}^{ab}\bar{P}_{a}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{bc})\,.

Finally, vanishing of
\operatorname{En}[\bar{A}]
in dimension
d=6
follows directly from that of
Q[\mbox{\boldmath{$g$}},A]
. The vanishing property
\operatorname{En}[\bar{A}]
for
d\in\{8,10,\ldots\}
follows from a simple evenness argument based on a canonical expansion of the connection; details are presented in Section
7.1
. (Note that the same method can likely be used to establish vanishing of the energy integrand
Q[\mbox{\boldmath{$g$}},A]=0
in those cases too.)
∎

Remark 6.4
.

Since
\partial\Sigma=\emptyset
, upon integrations by parts, the
d=7
energy integral takes the simple form

\overline{\operatorname{En}}[\bar{A}]=\frac{1}{8}\int_{\Sigma}{{\rm d}{\rm Vol}(\mbox{\boldmath$\bar{g}$})}\operatorname{Tr}\big({\bar{\jmath}}^{2}+\bar{J}{\bar{F}}^{2}+4{\bar{F}}^{ab}\bar{P}_{a}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{bc}\big)\,.

\blacklozenge

6.2.
The Obstruction

Our energy computations allow us to
employ the variational result Theorem
1.10
to efficiently compute the obstruction current
\bar{k}

of Equation (
1.7
).
The for this result was recorded in Theorem
1.11
which is proved below.

Proof of Theorem
1.11
.

The first three results quoted (save for their overall normalizations) follow from simple variational calculations: in
d=5
the boundary energy is the standard Yang–Mills functional, when
d=6
the energy vanishes. The
d=7
case follows from a straightforward variational computation that we leave for the reader. (We give an alternate recursion-based method for computing general obstruction currents and their normalizations by constants below.) The vanishing of
\bar{k}
in
d=8,10,12,\ldots
follows from vanishing of the energy in those dimensions as established in Theorem
1.9
along with
the variational property of the obstruction current given in Theorem
1.10
.
A direct proof based on evenness properties of the connection expansion is discussed
in Section
7.1
.
∎

Remark 6.5
.

Since the obstruction current is variational, a simple check of the above results is that the divergence of the obstruction current
\bar{\nabla}^{a}\bar{k}_{a}
vanishes identically.

\blacklozenge

We now develop an algorithm for computing the obstruction current—in principle for any dimension
d
—without relying on knowledge of the energy
\overline{\operatorname{En}}[\bar{A}]
. The basic idea is a direct generalization of the recursion developed in
[
28
]
for computing the obstruction to solving the singular Yamabe problem of finding a defining density such that the corresponding scalar curvature
Sc^{{\mathring{g}}}=-d(d-1)
. To begin with, we observe from Equation (
1.6
) that calculating the obstruction current
\bar{k}
requires computation of
d-4
normal derivatives
\nabla_{n}^{d-4}j
of the conformally compact Yang–Mills current along
\Sigma
. That requires knowledge of successive normal derivatives of the asymptotically Yang–Mills connection or its curvature. The following recursion is organized order by order in the transverse degree of the jets of the curvature and the dimension; see
[
6
]
for a detailed definition of the transverse degree of tensors on conformally compact structures.
Note that
F_{nb}
and
\nabla^{a}F_{ab}

have transverse degrees one and two respectively.
We now give a key technical result for normal derivatives of
j
.

Proposition 6.6
.

Let
(M^{d},\boldsymbol{c},\sigma)
be asymptotically Poincaré–Einstein with
d\geq 5
, and
\nabla^{A}
an asymptotically Yang–Mills connection. Also let
1\leq\ell\leq d-4
. Then the corresponding conformally compact Yang–Mills current
j
obeys

(6.6)

\nabla_{n}^{\ell}j_{b}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}(\ell-d+4)\nabla_{n}^{\ell-1}\nabla^{a}F_{ab}+{\rm LTOTs}\,,

where
{\rm LTOTs}
denotes terms of lower transverse degree.

Proof.

We first compute

\nabla_{n}^{\ell}j_{b}=\nabla^{\ell}_{n}\big(\sigma\nabla^{a}F_{ab}-(d-4)F_{nb}\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}{\ell}\nabla_{n}^{{\ell}-1}\nabla^{a}F_{ab}-(d-4)\nabla_{n}^{\ell}F_{nb}+{\rm LTOTs}\,.

Here we used that
F_{nb}
and
\nabla^{a}F_{ab}

have respective transverse degrees one and two and that
\nabla_{n}^{\ell}\circ\sigma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\ell\nabla_{n}^{\ell-1}
. Also, the operator
\nabla_{n}
increases the transverse degree of a tensor by one.
Now observe that

\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla_{n}^{{\ell}-1}F_{nb}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{c}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{n}^{{\ell}-1}F_{cb}+{\rm LTOTs}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\nabla^{a}\nabla_{n}^{{\ell}-1}F_{ab}-\nabla^{{\hskip 0.85358pt}\top}_{a}\nabla_{n}^{{\ell}-1}F^{a}{}_{b}+{\rm LTOTs}\,.

In the above we have used that
\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\bar{g}^{ab}-g^{ab}
and the operator identity
\bar{g}^{ab}\nabla_{b}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\nabla^{\top}_{b}
. Noting that
\nabla^{{\hskip 0.85358pt}\top}_{a}\nabla_{n}^{{\ell}-1}F^{a}{}_{b}
has lower transverse degree than
\nabla^{a}\nabla_{n}^{{\ell}-1}F_{ab}
as well as simple algebra, gives the result.
∎

The seemingly innocuous result above generates a recursion, because when
\ell=d-4
, the terms denoted “LTOTs” in Equation (
6.6
) give
(d-4)!
times the obstruction current. However, since these are of lower transverse degree they will have been computed in a previous step. For
\ell=1,\ldots,d-5
, the left hand side of Equation (
6.6
) vanishes because
\nabla^{A}
is asymptotically Yang–Mills and
\nabla_{n}^{\ell}\circ\sigma^{d-4}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}0
when
\ell<d-4
. In that case, the terms LTOTs give an expression for
\nabla_{n}^{\ell-1}\nabla^{a}F_{ab}
.
This information is precisely that needed to compute the LTOTs in the following step.

Let us now perform the recursion explicitly to give a non-variational proof of the
d=5,6,7
results of Theorem
1.11
.
Although the following lemma is a special case of Proposition (
1.11
), we separate it from the
d=6,7
cases, to better illustrate the recursion.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
(M^{d},\boldsymbol{c},\sigma)
be asymptotically Poincaré–Einstein with
d=5
and
\nabla^{A}
an asymptotically Yang–Mills connection. Then
the obstruction current
\bar{k}

equals the boundary Yang–Mills current
{\bar{\jmath}}
.

Proof.

When
d=5
, Equation (
1.6
) for an asymptotically Yang–Mills connection reads

\sigma\nabla^{a}F_{ab}-F_{nb}=\sigma k_{b}\,.

The obstruction
\bar{k}
is
k|_{\Sigma}
.
Hence, acting with
\nabla_{n}
and restricting to
\Sigma
we have

\bar{k}_{b}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\nabla^{a}F_{ab}-\nabla_{\hat{n}}F_{nb}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\nabla^{a}F_{ab}+(\bar{g}^{ac}-g^{ac})\nabla_{a}F_{cb}=\bar{g}^{ac}\nabla^{\top}_{a}F_{cb}\,,

where the second equality relies on Equation (
5.16
) and
F_{nb}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}0
.
The cancellation of terms proportional to
\nabla^{a}F_{ab}
is precisely that predicted by Proposition (
6.6
). An application of Equation (
5.4
) yields the result because
\bar{\jmath}_{b}=\bar{\nabla}^{a}\bar{F}_{ab}
.
∎

We now turn to the case
d=6
.

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
(M^{d},\boldsymbol{c},\sigma)
be asymptotically Poincaré–Einstein with
d=6
, and
\nabla^{A}
an asymptotically Yang–Mills connection. Then
the obstruction current
\bar{k}
vanishes.

Proof.

We are now in the
\ell=2
case of Proposition
1.11
for which

\bar{k}_{b}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{n}^{2}\big(\sigma\nabla^{a}F_{ab}-2F_{nb}\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla^{a}F_{ab}+\frac{1}{2}(\nabla_{\hat{n}}n^{2})\nabla^{a}F_{ab}-\nabla_{n}^{2}F_{nb}\,.

The first equality above uses Equation (
1.6
), while the second relies on
\nabla_{n}\sigma=n^{2}
.
Here neither of Lemmas
5.33
nor
5.36
can be fruitfully employed. Instead we manipulate the last term displayed above directly following the method of Proposition
1.11
:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla_{n}F_{nb}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\ \hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{c}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{n}F_{cb}+2H\hat{n}^{c}\nabla_{\hat{n}}F_{cb}+(\nabla_{n}^{2}n^{c})\bar{F}_{cb}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\ (\nabla_{a}-\nabla_{a}^{\top}+2H\hat{n}_{a}){\hskip 0.85358pt}\nabla_{n}F^{a}{}_{b}-P_{\hat{n}}{}^{a}\bar{F}_{ab}\,.

Note that the first equality above used Equation (
5.17
) while the second relied on Equation (
5.19
).
Thus
