\displaystyle\bar{k}_{b}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\

\displaystyle[\nabla_{n},\nabla^{a}]F_{ab}+\nabla^{a\top}\big(\hat{n}_{a}\bar{\jmath}_{b}-\hat{n}_{b}\bar{\jmath}_{a}-2H\bar{F}_{ab})+P_{\hat{n}}{}^{a}\bar{F}_{ab}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\

\displaystyle-Ric_{\hat{n}}{}^{a}{\bar{F}}_{ab}+R_{\hat{n}}{}^{a}{}_{b}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{ac}\!-(\nabla^{a}n^{c})\nabla_{c}F_{ab}+2H\bar{\jmath}_{b}\!-\hat{n}_{b}\bar{\nabla}^{a}\bar{\jmath}_{a}\!-2(\bar{\nabla}^{a}H)\bar{F}_{ab}\!+P_{\hat{n}}{}^{a}\bar{F}_{ab}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\

\displaystyle-2(\bar{\nabla}^{a}H+P_{\hat{n}}{}^{a})\bar{F}_{ab}=0\,.

The first equality above used Equations (
5.31
,
5.32
)
and
\nabla_{\hat{n}}n^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}2H
(see Equation (
5.17
)). The second relied on Equation (
5.14
).
The penultimate equality needed the standard relation between Ricci and Schouten tensors, Equation (
5.5
), Equation (
5.16
) and the Bianchi identity
\bar{\nabla}^{a}\bar{\jmath}_{a}=0
.
The last equality used the identity
P_{\hat{n}a}^{\top}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}-\bar{\nabla}_{a}H
, valid for Poincaré–Einstein embeddings; see Equation (
5.8
).
∎

The
d=7
case is considerably more involved but provides a nice check of the machinery developed here.

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
(M^{d},\boldsymbol{c},\sigma)
be asymptotically Poincaré–Einstein with
d=7
, and
\nabla^{A}
an asymptotically Yang–Mills connection. Then
the obstruction current is given by

\bar{k}=\frac{1}{2}\bar{\nabla}^{a}\big(\bar{\nabla}_{[a}{\bar{\jmath}}_{b]}-4\bar{P}_{[a}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{b]c}-\bar{J}\bar{F}_{ab}\big)+\frac{1}{4}[\bar{\jmath}^{a},\bar{F}_{ab}]\,.

Proof.

Dimension
d=7
is the
\ell=3
case of Proposition
1.11
so we must compute
\frac{1}{3!}\nabla_{n}^{3}\big(\sigma\nabla^{a}F_{ab}-3F_{nb}\big)
along
\Sigma
, and thus begin with the first term

\displaystyle\nabla_{n}^{3}\big(\sigma\nabla^{a}F_{ab}\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ 3\nabla_{n}^{2}\nabla^{a}F_{ab}-2\nabla_{n}(\rho\sigma\nabla_{n}\nabla^{a}F_{ab})-2\nabla_{n}^{2}(\rho\sigma\nabla^{a}F_{ab})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ 3\nabla_{n}^{2}\nabla^{a}F_{ab}+6H\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla^{a}F_{ab}-2\big(\nabla_{n}^{2}(\rho\sigma)\big)\nabla^{a}F_{ab}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ 3\nabla_{n}^{2}\nabla^{a}F_{ab}-18HP_{\hat{n}}{}^{a}\bar{F}_{ab}-12H^{2}\bar{\jmath}_{b}-6P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}\bar{\jmath}_{b}\,.

The first line is a simple application of the Leibniz rule and
\nabla_{n}\sigma=n^{2}=1-2\rho\sigma+{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma^{5})
, while the second line uses that
\rho\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}-H
.
For the third line we used that

\nabla_{n}^{2}(\rho\sigma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\nabla_{n}\rho+\nabla_{n}(n^{2}\rho)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}2P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}-2H^{2}
(see Equation (
5.18
)).

Now we must handle three normal derivatives of
F_{nb}
. Again, the key idea is that of Theorem
1.11
, namely to replace a pair of unit conormals with the difference of ambient and hypersurface metrics:

\displaystyle-3\nabla_{n}^{3}F_{nb}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ -3\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{c}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{n}^{2}F_{cb}-3\big[\nabla_{n}^{3},n^{c}\big]F_{cb}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ -3\nabla^{a}\nabla_{n}^{2}F_{ab}+3\nabla_{a}^{\top}\nabla_{n}^{2}F^{a}{}_{b}-3(\nabla_{n}^{3}n^{c})\bar{F}_{cb}-9(\nabla_{n}^{2}n^{c})\nabla_{\hat{n}}F_{cb}-9\hat{n}^{c}H\nabla_{n}^{2}F_{cb}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ -3\nabla^{a}\nabla_{n}^{2}F_{ab}+3\bar{\nabla}^{a}\big({\hskip 0.85358pt}\bar{\nabla}_{[a}\bar{\jmath}_{b]}-2\bar{P}_{[a}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{b]c}+(2P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}+3H^{2})\bar{F}_{ab}\big)

\displaystyle\ +15H\big(P_{\hat{n}}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{bc}-2H\bar{\jmath}_{b}\big)-3\hat{n}_{b}\bar{\nabla}^{a}\big(P_{\hat{n}}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{ac}-2H\bar{\jmath}_{a}\big)

\displaystyle\ +3\big(2(\bar{\nabla}^{a}P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}})+HP_{\hat{n}}{}^{a}\big)\bar{F}_{ab}-9\Big(\tfrac{1}{2}\big(H^{2}-2P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}})\bar{\jmath}_{b}+\tfrac{1}{2}\hat{n}_{b}P_{\hat{n}}{}^{a}\bar{\jmath}_{a}+2HP_{\hat{n}}{}^{a}\bar{F}_{ab}\Big)

\displaystyle\ -9H\big(P_{\hat{n}}{}^{a}\bar{F}_{ba}-2H\bar{\jmath}_{b}\big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ -3\nabla^{a}\nabla_{n}^{2}F_{ab}+3\bar{\nabla}^{a}\big({\hskip 0.85358pt}\bar{\nabla}_{[a}\bar{\jmath}_{b]}-2\bar{P}_{[a}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{b]c}\big)

\displaystyle\ +12(\bar{\nabla}^{a}P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}})\bar{F}_{ab}+15P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}\bar{\jmath}_{b}-39HP_{\hat{n}}{}^{a}\bar{F}_{ab}-\frac{15}{2}H^{2}\bar{\jmath}_{b}-\frac{27}{2}\hat{n}_{b}P_{\hat{n}}{}^{a}\bar{\jmath}_{a}\,.

The second line above used Equation (
5.17
).
To achieve the third line, we observe that Equation (
5.36
)
may be employed under a tangential derivative
\nabla^{\top}
. That line also heavily relies on Lemma
5.2
as well as Equation (
5.31
).
The last line above uses that the divergence of the boundary curvature
\bar{F}
gives the current
\bar{j}
as well as Equation
5.8
.

Now, by writing
-3\nabla^{a}\nabla_{n}^{2}F_{ab}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\ -3\nabla_{n}^{2}\nabla^{a}F_{ab}-3\big[\nabla^{a},\nabla_{n}^{2}]F_{ab}
all of the
\nabla_{n}^{2}\nabla^{a}F_{ab}
terms of highest transverse degree cancel (again this is the general phenomenon established in Theorem
1.11
) but we must still compute the lower order commutator terms:

\displaystyle-3\big[\nabla^{a},

\displaystyle\nabla_{n}^{2}]F_{ab}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\ -{\hskip 0.85358pt}3Ric_{\hat{n}}{}^{c}\nabla_{\hat{n}}F_{cb}-3R^{a}{}_{\hat{n}b}{}^{c}\nabla_{\hat{n}}F_{ac}-3(\nabla^{a}n^{c})\nabla_{c}\nabla_{n}F_{ab}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\>\>-3\nabla_{\hat{n}}\big(Ric{}_{n}{}^{c}F_{cb}+R^{a}{}_{nb}{}^{c}F_{ac}+\big[F^{a}{}_{n},F_{ab}\big]+(\nabla^{a}n^{c})\nabla_{c}F_{ab}\big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ -6Ric_{\hat{n}}{}^{c}\nabla_{\hat{n}}F_{cb}-6R^{a}{}_{\hat{n}b}{}^{c}\nabla_{\hat{n}}F_{ac}-6H\nabla^{a}\nabla_{n}F_{ab}

\displaystyle\ -3(\nabla_{\hat{n}}Ric_{n}{}^{c})\bar{F}_{cb}-3(\nabla_{\hat{n}}R^{a}{}_{nb}{}^{c})\bar{F}_{ac}-3\big[(\nabla_{\hat{n}}F^{a}{}_{n}),\bar{F}_{ab}\big]

\displaystyle\ -3(\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla^{a}n^{c})\nabla_{c}F_{ab}-3H[\nabla_{n},\nabla^{a}]F_{ab}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ -3(5P_{\hat{n}}{}^{a}+J\hat{n}^{a})\big(\hat{n}_{a}\bar{\jmath}_{b}-\hat{n}_{b}\bar{\jmath}_{a}-4H\bar{F}_{ab}\big)

\displaystyle\ -3P_{\hat{n}}{}^{c}\big(\hat{n}_{b}\bar{\jmath}_{c}-\hat{n}_{c}\bar{\jmath}_{b}-4H\bar{F}_{bc}\big)-3P^{a}{}_{b}\hat{n}^{c}\big(\hat{n}_{a}\bar{\jmath}_{c}-\hat{n}_{c}\bar{\jmath}_{a}-4H\bar{F}_{ac}\big)

\displaystyle\ -3H\nabla_{a}^{\top}\big(\hat{n}^{a}\bar{\jmath}_{b}-\hat{n}_{b}\bar{\jmath}^{a}-4H\bar{F}^{a}{}_{b})-6H\big(P_{\hat{n}}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{bc}-2H\bar{\jmath}_{b}\big)

\displaystyle\ -15HP_{\hat{n}}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{cb}-15\hat{n}^{a}(\nabla_{\hat{n}}P_{a}{}^{c})\bar{F}_{cb}-3HP_{\hat{n}}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{bc}-3\hat{n}^{d}(\nabla_{\hat{n}}R^{a}{}_{db}{}^{c})\bar{F}_{ac}

\displaystyle\ +\frac{3}{2}\big[\bar{\jmath}^{a},\bar{F}_{ab}\big]\!+\!3\big(P^{ac}\!+\!g^{ac}P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}\big)\nabla_{c}F_{ab}+3HRic_{\hat{n}}{}^{a}\bar{F}_{ab}\!-\!3HR_{\hat{n}}{}^{a}{}_{b}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{ac}\!+\frac{9}{2}H^{2}\bar{\jmath}_{b}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ -3\bar{J}{\hskip 0.85358pt}\bar{\jmath}_{b}+3\bar{P}^{a}{}_{b}\bar{\jmath}_{a}+\frac{3}{2}\big[\bar{\jmath}^{a},\bar{F}_{ab}\big]-\frac{21}{2}P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}\bar{\jmath}_{b}+42HP_{\hat{n}}{}^{a}\bar{F}_{ab}+21H^{2}\bar{\jmath}_{b}

\displaystyle\ +15\hat{n}_{b}P_{\hat{n}}{}^{a}\bar{\jmath}_{a}-15\hat{n}^{a}(\nabla_{\hat{n}}P_{a}{}^{c})\bar{F}_{cb}-3\hat{n}^{d}(\nabla_{\hat{n}}R^{a}{}_{db}{}^{c})\bar{F}_{ac}+3P^{ac}\nabla_{c}F_{ab}\,.

The first line above used the Leibniz property of the operator
[\nabla_{n},{\boldsymbol{\hbox to2.5pt{\hss$\cdot$\hss}}}{\hskip 0.85358pt}]
while the second line distributed the operator
\nabla_{\hat{n}}
and used that
F|_{\Sigma}=\bar{F}
. The third equality used Equations (
5.30
,
5.31
,
5.36
,
5.20
), the results of which are collated in the last line.
It remains to develop the terms above involving normal derivatives of ambient curvatures. Firstly

\hat{n}^{a}(\nabla_{\hat{n}}P_{a}{}^{c})\bar{F}_{cb}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}(\bar{\nabla}^{c}J)\bar{F}_{cb}-(\nabla_{a}^{\top}P^{ac})\bar{F}_{cb}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}-2HP_{\hat{n}}{}^{a}\bar{F}_{ab}+(\bar{\nabla}^{a}P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}})\bar{F}_{ab}\,.

Here again we have used the usual maneuver for handling pairs of conormals as well as Equations (
5.7
) and (
5.8
).
Along similar lines, we also compute

\displaystyle\hat{n}^{d}(\nabla_{\hat{n}}R^{a}{}_{db}{}^{c})\bar{F}_{ac}\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\

\displaystyle(\nabla^{c}Ric^{a}{}_{b})\bar{F}_{ac}-(\nabla_{d}^{\top}R^{ad}{}_{b}{}^{c})\bar{F}_{ac}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\

\displaystyle\frac{5}{2}C^{ca}{}_{b}\bar{F}_{ac}+\big(\bar{\nabla}^{a}(\bar{J}+P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}-3H^{2})\big)\bar{F}_{ba}-(\nabla^{\top}_{d}W^{ad}{}_{b}{}^{c})\bar{F}_{ac}

\displaystyle\ -(\nabla^{\top}_{d}P^{dc})\bar{F}_{bc}-(\nabla^{\top}_{c}P^{a}{}_{b})\bar{F}_{ac}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\

\displaystyle-\frac{5}{2}\bar{C}_{acb}\bar{F}^{ac}+\frac{5}{2}\hat{n}_{b}C_{ac\hat{n}}\bar{F}^{ac}-(\bar{\nabla}^{a}\bar{J})\bar{F}_{ab}-(\bar{\nabla}^{a}P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}})\bar{F}_{ab}-6HP_{\hat{n}}{}^{a}\bar{F}_{ab}

\displaystyle\ +3\bar{C}_{bca}\bar{F}^{ac}+(\bar{\nabla}^{a}\bar{J})\bar{F}_{ab}+8HP_{\hat{n}}{}^{a}\bar{F}_{ab}-\frac{1}{2}\bar{C}_{cab}\bar{F}^{ac}+HP_{\hat{n}}{}^{a}\bar{F}_{ab}

\displaystyle\ -\hat{n}_{b}(\bar{\nabla}_{c}P_{\hat{n}a}^{\top})\bar{F}^{ac}-HP_{\hat{n}}{}^{a}\bar{F}_{ab}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\bar{C}_{acb}\bar{F}^{ac}-(\bar{\nabla}^{a}P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}})\bar{F}_{ab}+2HP_{\hat{n}}{}^{a}\bar{F}_{ab}\,.

The second line above relied on the definitions of the Schouten and Cotton tensors; see Equations (
1.11
) and (
1.13
).
We also employed Equation (
5.7
) and the decomposition of the Riemann tensor into Weyl and Schouten tensors in Equation (
1.12
).
In the third line we used Equation (
5.13
),
as well as Equation (
5.12
) in combination with the result for the divergence of the Weyl tensor in Equation (
1.14
). For the last line we used Equation (
5.9
) as well as the vanishing of
C_{ac\hat{n}}
by virtue of Equations (
5.5
) and (
1.14
) (see also Lemma
5.1
).
One further computation is needed

P^{ac}\nabla_{c}F_{ab}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}P^{ac}\nabla_{c}^{\top}\bar{F}_{ab}+P_{\hat{n}}{}^{a}\nabla_{\hat{n}}F_{ab}\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}P^{ac}(\bar{\nabla}_{c}\bar{F}_{ab}-H\hat{n}_{a}\bar{F}_{cb})+P_{\hat{n}}{}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}\hat{n}_{a}\bar{\jmath}_{b}-\tfrac{1}{2}\hat{n}_{b}\bar{\jmath}_{a}-2H\bar{F}_{ab}\big)\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\bar{P}^{ac}\bar{\nabla}_{c}\bar{F}_{ab}-\frac{1}{2}H^{2}\bar{\jmath}_{b}-3HP_{\hat{n}}{}^{a}\bar{F}_{ab}+\frac{1}{2}P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}\bar{\jmath}_{b}-\frac{1}{2}\hat{n}_{b}P_{\hat{n}}{}^{a}\bar{\jmath}_{a}\,.

In the above we decomposed
\nabla\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\nabla^{\top}+\hat{n}\nabla_{\hat{n}}
and subsequently employed Equation (
5.31
) as well as Equation (
5.4
) to convert
\nabla^{\top}
to
\bar{\nabla}
plus terms involving mean curvature.
The above calculation is completed using Equation (
5.7
).

Orchestrating all these computations gives

\quad\frac{1}{3}\nabla_{n}^{3}\big(\sigma\nabla^{a}F_{ab}-3F_{nb}\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\bar{\nabla}^{a}\big({\hskip 0.85358pt}\bar{\nabla}_{[a}\bar{\jmath}_{b]}-2\bar{P}_{[a}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{b]c}\big)-\bar{J}{\hskip 0.85358pt}\bar{\jmath}_{b}+\bar{P}^{a}{}_{b}\bar{\jmath}_{a}+\frac{1}{2}\big[\bar{\jmath}^{a},\bar{F}_{ab}\big]\\
+\frac{1}{2}\bar{C}_{acb}\bar{F}^{ac}+\bar{P}^{ac}\bar{\nabla}_{c}\bar{F}_{ab}\,.

It is not difficult to verify that

\bar{P}^{a}{}_{b}\bar{\jmath}_{a}+\frac{1}{2}\bar{C}_{acb}\bar{F}^{ac}+\bar{P}^{ac}\bar{\nabla}_{c}\bar{F}_{ab}=-2\bar{\nabla}^{a}(\bar{P}_{[a}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{b]c})-(\bar{\nabla}^{a}\bar{J})F_{ab}\,.

Remembering that
\bar{\jmath}_{b}=\bar{\nabla}^{a}\bar{F}_{ab}
, it follows that
\frac{1}{3!}\nabla_{n}^{3}j|_{\Sigma}
equals the result quoted for the obstruction current
\bar{k}
.
∎

7.
Boundary Operators and Dirichlet-to-Neumann Maps

To construct Dirichlet-to-Neumann
maps for the Yang–Mills system, we rely on a canonical expansion of the connection generalizing those given for the Maxwell
and Poincaré–Einstein systems respectively in Sections
3.1
and
5.1
.
This expansion also allows us to complete the proofs of Theorems
1.9
and
1.11
.

7.1.
Canonical Connection Expansion

To study the asymptotics of conformally compact Yang–Mills connections on Poincaré–Einstein structures
(M,\boldsymbol{c},\sigma)
,
we first express the conformally compact Yang–Mills equation (
3.3
) in the Graham–Lee coordinate system
(x,r)
for a collar neighborhood of
\Sigma
. Those coordinates are
predicated on a choice of boundary metric
\bar{h}\in\boldsymbol{c}_{\Sigma}
; see Section
5.1
. Since the density
\sigma=[{\rm d}r^{2}+h(x,r),r]
in the collar, we work in the scale determined by the choice of compactified metric
{\rm d}r^{2}+h(x,r)
.
We also choose frames
\Phi
for the bundle
{\mathcal{V}}M
so that the connection
\nabla^{A}={\rm d}+A^{\Phi}
, where
A^{\Phi}
is an endomorphism-valued one-form.

It is propitious to make a “temporal gauge choice”, meaning frames
\Phi
such that

A^{\Phi}_{0}:=\iota_{\frac{\partial}{\partial r}}A^{\Phi}=A^{\Phi}_{n}=0\,.

To see that such a choice is possible, note that

A^{U\Phi}_{0}\sim U^{-1}U^{\prime}+U^{-1}A^{\Phi}_{0}U\,,

where
U
is any smooth endomorphism.
Also, primes denote derivatives with respect to
r
. Thus we need to solve the linear ordinary differential equation

U^{\prime}=-A^{\Phi}_{0}U\,,

for
U
given
A^{\Phi}_{0}
. We take as initial data for
U
the condition
U|_{r=0}=\operatorname{Id}
. Clearly this system always has a solution for a sufficiently small collar neighborhood of the boundary.
Having fixed a “choice of gauge”, from now on we drop the label
\Phi
on the endomorphism-valued one-form
A
trusting that no confusion this with the same label
A
used to denote the connection
\nabla^{A}
will arise.

We now employ an index notation
i=1,\ldots,d-1
for the coordinates
x=(x^{i})
and recycle the abstract indices
a,b,c,\ldots
to denote the collar coordinates
y^{a}=(x^{i},r)
.
Then, thanks to the above gauge choice, the curvature two-form is simply

F={\rm d}r\wedge A^{\prime}+\hat{F}\,,

where
A=A_{i}(x,r){\rm d}x^{i}
and
\hat{F}=\frac{1}{2}{\rm d}x^{i}\wedge{\rm d}x^{j}F_{ij}(x,r)
with

F_{ij}=\partial_{i}A_{j}(x,r)-\partial_{j}A_{i}(x,r)+\big[A_{i}(x,r),A_{j}(x,r)\big]\,.

In this scale and coordinate system, the covector
n={\rm d}r
, so

(7.1)

F_{nb}{\rm d}y^{b}=A^{\prime}_{j}{\rm d}x^{j}\,.

The divergence of the curvature is given by

(\nabla^{a}F_{ab}){\rm d}y^{b}=-\hat{\nabla}^{i}A^{\prime}_{i}\,{\rm d}r+(A^{\prime\prime}_{j}+\hat{\nabla}^{i}\hat{F}_{ij}+\tfrac{1}{2}h^{\prime i}{}_{i}A^{\prime}_{j}-h^{\prime i}{}_{j}A^{\prime}_{i}){\rm d}x^{j}\,.

Here we have denoted by
\hat{\nabla}
the Yang–Mills coupled connection along constant
r
hypersurfaces so that, for a one-form valued endomorphism
X_{j}(x,r)
, one has

\hat{\nabla}_{i}X_{j}=\partial_{i}X_{j}-\hat{\Gamma}_{ij}^{k}X_{k}+[A_{i},X_{j}]\,.

We raise and lower indices
i,j,k,\ldots
with the hypersurface metric
h_{ij}(x,r)
.
Also we have used that the Christoffel symbols of the metric
{\rm d}r^{2}+h(x,r)
are

\Gamma^{0}_{00}=0=\Gamma^{0}_{0j}=\Gamma_{00}^{k}
,

\Gamma^{k}_{0j}=\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime k}{}_{j}
,

\Gamma^{0}_{ij}=-\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime}_{ij}

and

\Gamma^{k}_{ij}=\hat{\Gamma}^{k}_{ij}
.
Thus the conformally compact Yang–Mills equations given in (
3.2
) become

(7.2)

\displaystyle j_{0}[A]

\displaystyle:=\,-r\hat{\nabla}^{i}A^{\prime}_{i}=0\,,

(7.3)

\displaystyle j_{j}[A]

\displaystyle:=\,\Big(r\frac{\partial}{\partial r}-d+4\Big)A^{\prime}_{j}+r\hat{\nabla}^{i}\hat{F}_{ij}+\tfrac{1}{2}rh^{\prime i}{}_{i}A^{\prime}_{j}-rh^{\prime i}{}_{j}A^{\prime}_{i}=0\,.

Let us call the first of these the Gauß law because
A^{\prime}_{i}
is the Yang–Mills analog of an electric field. We dub the second equation the Ampère law, again in analogy with electromagnetism.

We now seek a formal solution in the form

(7.4)

A_{j}(x,r)=A^{(0)}_{j}(x)+rA^{(1)}_{j}(x)+r^{2}A^{(2)}_{j}(x)+\cdots\,.

In what follows, recall that
h(x,r)
obeys the expansion given in Equation (
5.26
).
The Ampère law at order
r^{0}
, when
d>4
, implies that

(7.5)

A^{(1)}=0.

It is known (see for example
[
18
,
30
]
) that the order
r^{2}
term in the asymptotic expansion for the metric
g
is given by

h_{ij}^{(2)}(X)=-\hat{P}_{ij}(x)\,,

where
\hat{P}_{ij}
is the Schouten tensor of the boundary metric
h^{(0)}(x)
and
\hat{J}
denotes
\hat{P}^{i}{}_{i}
. Note that
\hat{P}_{ij}(x)
is the boundary Schouten tensor
\bar{P}_{ij}
in the boundary coordinate system
x^{i}
.
Hence at order
r^{k}
,
assuming an expansion of
A_{i}
of the form (
7.4
),
the Ampère Law (
7.3
) gives

(7.6)

0=(k+1)(k-d+4)A_{j}^{(k+1)}+\big(\bar{\Delta}-(k-1)\bar{J}\big)A_{j}^{(k-1)}\\
-\big(\bar{\nabla}^{i}\bar{\nabla}_{j}-2(k-1)\bar{P}^{i}{}_{j}\big)A_{i}^{(k-1)}+\big[A_{i}^{(k-1)},\bar{F}^{i}{}_{j}\big]+\mbox{LOTs}\,,

where LOTs denotes terms involving
A^{(\ell)}
with
\ell<k-1
, and quantities with bars are evaluated along the boundary at
r=0
.
From the above we find that

A^{\rm ev}_{i}(x,r)=A^{(0)}_{i}(x)+rA^{(1)}_{i}(x)+r^{2}A^{(2)}_{i}(x)+\cdots+r^{d-4}A_{i}^{(d-4)}(x)\,,

solving

(7.7)

j_{i}[A^{\rm ev}]={\mathcal{O}}(r^{d-4})\,,

is such that each
A^{(\ell)}_{i}(x)
for
1\leq\ell\leq d-4
is uniquely determined in terms of
A^{(0)}_{i}(x)
. Moreover, since the metric expansion of
h(x,r)
is even to order
r^{d-2}
, by inspecting Equations (
7.5
) and (
7.6
), we see that the Ampère law inductively implies that all terms
A^{(\ell)}(x)
with
\ell\leq d-4
and odd must vanish, and thus
A_{i}^{\rm ev}(x,r)
is an even polynomial function of
r
.

We still have to impose the Gauß law. For that we note that the Bianchi identity in the form of Equation (
3.4
) applied to
j[A^{\rm ev}]
in tandem with Equation (
7.7
) gives

(7.8)

\Big(r\frac{\partial}{\partial r}-d+3\Big)j_{0}=-r\hat{\nabla}^{i}j_{i}-\frac{1}{2}rh^{ij}h^{\prime}_{ij}j_{0}=-\frac{1}{2}rh^{ij}h^{\prime}_{ij}j_{0}+{\mathcal{O}}(r^{d-3})\,,

where
j_{0}=-r\hat{\nabla}^{i}A^{\prime\rm ev}_{i}
is an even polynomial in
r
of degree at most
d-4
plus terms of order
r^{d-3}
(potentially involving logs coming from the metric expansion).
A simple induction again shows that
j_{0}[A^{\rm ev}]={\mathcal{O}}(r^{d-3})
.
Hence we have established that
A^{\rm ev}
satisfies

(7.9)

j[A^{\rm ev}]={\mathcal{O}}(r^{d-4})\kappa(x,r)+{\mathcal{O}}(r^{d-3}){\hskip 0.85358pt}{\rm d}r\,,

where
\kappa
is some smooth endomorphism-valued one-form in the kernel of
\iota_{\frac{\partial}{\partial r}}
.
Hence
j_{i}[A^{\rm ev}]=r^{d-4}k_{i}(x)+{\mathcal{O}}(r^{d-3})
where
k_{i}(x)
is determined by the boundary connection and choice of boundary metric
\bar{h}\in\boldsymbol{c}_{\Sigma}
.
In
[
30
]
it was shown that upon changing the choice of boundary metric representative
\bar{h}
to
\bar{\Omega}^{2}\bar{h}
, the compactified metric
g
changes to
\Omega(x,r)^{2}g
where
\Omega(x,r)
has an even expansion in
r
and obeys
\Omega(x,0)=\bar{\Omega}
. Also the new canonical coordinate is
\Omega(x,r){\hskip 0.85358pt}r
. It follows that, under a change of boundary metric representative,
k_{i}
indeed transforms as a weight
3-d
density-valued one-form and thus gives the obstruction current
\bar{k}
in the boundary scale determined by
\bar{h}
.
This is a special case of our general result Theorem
1.2
for conformally compact structures.
In other words,
A^{\rm ev}
is an asymptotically Yang–Mills connection expressed in a distinguished coordinate system and we have now reproduced Theorem
1.2
, but with the additional information that the expansion
in the canonical coordinate
r
is even up to order
r^{d-4}
when the background is specialized to a Poincaré–Einstein structure.

Note also that the terms in
Equation (
7.8
) of order
r^{d-3}
together with Equation (
7.9
) imply

0=\bar{\nabla}^{i}\bar{k}_{i}\,.

This establishes that the obstruction current
\bar{k}
is divergence-free along the boundary, which also follows from our earlier result that it is the variation of a boundary energy functional; see Theorem
1.10
.

We can now give the details behind the evenness arguments appealed to in the proofs of Theorems
1.8
,
1.9
, and
1.11
.
Theorem
1.8
states that the renormalized action
S^{\rm ren}[A]
is conformally invariant for Poincaré–Einstein structures. Its detailed proof mirrors exactly that given for renormalized volumes in
[
30
]
. Again we rely on the evenness property
of the new canonical coordinate
\Omega(x,r){\hskip 0.85358pt}r
corresponding to
changing the choice of boundary metric representative
\bar{h}
to
\bar{\Omega}^{2}\bar{h}
. In particular
the function
e^{\omega}
giving the change of scale in Theorem
1.8

equals
\Omega(x,r)
.
It is now easy to check that both the metric and connection expansions are even to
sufficiently high enough order that there can be no change in
S^{\rm ren}[A]
when varying the choice of boundary metric
\bar{h}
, which yields the result stated in Theorem
1.8
.

Theorems
1.9
, and
1.11
give vanishing results for the energy and obstruction in even boundary dimensions.
For those we must study the boundary asymptotics of the regulated Yang–Mills energy functional

(7.10)

S^{\varepsilon}[A^{\rm ev}]:=-\frac{1}{4}\int_{M^{\varepsilon}}{\rm d}{\rm Vol}({\mathring{g}})\,{{\rm Tr}}\big({\mathring{g}}^{ab}{\mathring{g}}^{cd}F^{A^{\rm ev}}_{ac}F^{A^{\rm ev}}_{bd}\big)\,,

evaluated on the asymptotic solution
\nabla^{A^{\rm ev}}
. We split the region into

M_{\varepsilon}=C_{\varepsilon}\cup M_{\rm int}\,,

where
C_{\varepsilon}
is the collar
\{p\in M\,|\,\varepsilon\leq r(p)\leq r^{*}\}
and
\varepsilon<r^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}
is some sufficiently small value such that the coordinate
r
is defined.
Since
M
and hence
\Sigma=\partial M
are compact,
such a value
r^{*}
exists. The region
M_{\rm int}:=M\backslash(C_{\varepsilon}\cup\{p\in M\,|\,r(p)<\varepsilon\})
.
To connect with the density-based approach to the regulated action functional in Section
6
, note that the density
\sigma
evaluated in the scale
g
is given by
\sigma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{=}}r
. Hence Equation (
6.1
) then determines the same region
M_{\varepsilon}
as that given above. Therefore the coefficients in the Laurent series of Theorem
1.6
equal those determined by the expansion made here.

In the above terms we have

S^{\varepsilon}=\int_{\varepsilon}^{r^{*}}\!\frac{{\rm d}r}{r^{d}}\,{\mathcal{L}}(r)-\frac{1}{4}\int_{M_{\rm int}}{\rm d}{\rm Vol}({\mathring{g}})\,{\rm Tr}\big({\mathring{g}}^{ab}{\mathring{g}}^{cd}F^{A^{\rm ev}}_{ac}F^{A^{\rm ev}}_{bd}\big)\,,

where
{\mathcal{L}}(r):=-\frac{1}{4}\int_{\Sigma_{r}}\!{\rm d}{\rm Vol}(h(r))\,{\mathring{g}}^{ac}{\mathring{g}}^{bd}{\hskip 0.85358pt}{{\rm Tr}}(F_{ab}{\hskip 0.85358pt}{\hskip 0.85358pt}F_{cd})
and

\Sigma_{r}
is a constant
r
hypersurface.
In even dimensions
d
, the integrand of the hypersurface integral
{\mathcal{L}}(r)
is an even polynomial in
r
plus terms of order
r^{d+1}
because
h(r)=h^{\rm ev}+{\mathcal{O}}(r^{d-1})
,
A=A^{\rm ev}+{\mathcal{O}}(r^{d-3})
and
{\mathring{g}}^{ab}=r^{2}g^{ab}
. Thus there is no contribution to the integrand of the above-displayed integral of order
r^{-1}
, so the coefficient of
\log\varepsilon
in the regulated Yang–Mills functional vanishes. Hence the energy functional
\overline{\operatorname{En}}[\bar{A}]={\rm En}[A^{\rm ev}[\bar{A}]]
of Equation (
1.10
)
vanishes in dimensions
d=6,8,10,\ldots
. This establishes the vanishing properties stated in the proofs of Theorems
1.9
and
1.11
.

It is also possible to show that the obstruction
\bar{k}
vanishes for even dimensional boundaries by directly examining the even connection expansion

A^{\rm ev}_{i}(x,r)=A^{(0)}_{i}(x)+r^{2}A^{(2)}_{i}(x)+\cdots+r^{d-4}A_{i}^{(d-4)}(x)\,,

and the Ampère equation (
7.3
), rather than relying on its variational property given in
Theorem
1.10
. Indeed
the boundary operators constructed in Section
7.2
below can be used to extract the above expansion coefficients.
See in particular Equation (
7.28
).
We have explicitly verified that when applied to the
regulated Yang–Mills energy functional (
7.10
), these yield the
energy functional
\overline{\operatorname{En}}[\bar{A}]
for six dimensional boundaries quoted in Theorem
1.9
. We suppress the details of those computations here.

7.2.
Yang–Mills Poincaré–Einstein Boundary Operators

We now want to construct Yang–Mills analogs of the boundary operators
\check{\delta}^{(2)}
and
\check{\delta}^{(3)}
in Proposition
5.4

on Poincaré–Einstein structures.
While the Neumann data
of the harmonic problem for scalars is respectively accessed by second and third order boundary operators in dimensions
d=3
and
d=4
, for the Yang–Mills system the corresponding ambient dimensions are
d=5
and
d=6
, while the
Neumann data
corresponds to transverse derivatives of the normal components of the field strength; see Sections
4.3
and
7.1
. Therefore we are interested in invariant covectors
{\sf E}^{(k)}_{b}\in\Gamma(T^{*}\Sigma\otimes{\mathcal{V}}\Sigma[-k])
of the form

\hat{n}^{a_{1}}\cdots\hat{n}^{a_{k}}(\nabla_{a_{1}}\cdots\nabla_{a_{k-1}}F_{a_{k}b})\big|_{\Sigma}+\cdots\,.

We may view

{\sf E}^{(k)}_{b}
as the image of a non-linear map from connections
\nabla^{A}
on
{\mathcal{V}}M
to invariant boundary covectors taking values in sections of
{\mathcal{V}}\Sigma[-k]
, and hence term

{\sf E}^{(k)}_{b}
a
Yang–Mills boundary operator
.
The integer
k
is called the
transverse order
of
{\sf E}^{(k)}
since it measures the maximal number of normal derivatives of the connection potential appearing in
{\sf E}^{(k)}
.
The ellipsis
\cdots
denotes terms of lower transverse order necessary for
{\sf E}^{(k)}_{b}
to be conformally invariant. We impose a naturalness condition on these terms, namely that they are built from (partial) contractions of connection-twisted covariant derivatives
\nabla^{A}
, curvatures
F^{A}
and
R
, the metric
g
and its inverse, as well as the defining density
\sigma
of the
Poincaré–Einstein structure

(M,\boldsymbol{c},\sigma)
. In Section
5
we explained how such quantities can be re-expressed in terms of tensors intrinsic to the boundary as well as extrinsic curvatures; see in particular Lemma
5.2
.

We are especially interested in constructing Yang–Mills boundary operators that vanish when evaluated on conformally compact Yang–Mills connections for sufficiently high dimension
d
at fixed values of
k
. This means that the image of these operators are obstructions to a connection
\nabla^{A}
solving the conformally compact Yang–Mills system.

Observe that a single normal derivative of the connection is accessed by contracting the curvature
F
with the unit normal
\hat{n}
, so we define

{\sf E}^{(1)}_{a}:=F_{\hat{n}a}\big|_{\Sigma}\in\Gamma(T^{*}\Sigma\otimes{\mathcal{V}}\Sigma[-1])\,.

This may be viewed as a Yang–Mills analog of the conformal-Robin operator
\delta^{(1)}
.
Clearly, for any connection
A
that obeys the conformally compact Yang–Mills condition (
1.3
), one has
{\sf E}^{(1)}_{a}=0
.

The following result is important for the transverse order
k=2
case.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
(M,\boldsymbol{c},\sigma)
be a Poincaré–Einstein structure
equipped with a smooth connection
\nabla^{A}
on
{\mathcal{V}}M
. Then

(7.11)

\check{\sf E}^{(2)}_{\,b}(g,A):=(d-5)\big[\hat{n}^{a}(\nabla_{\hat{n}}F_{ab})|_{\Sigma}+2H^{g}{\sf E}^{(1)}_{b}\big]\,-{\bar{\jmath}}_{b}\,

defines a section of
T^{*}\Sigma\otimes{\mathcal{V}}\Sigma[-2]
.

Proof.

First we may check that
\check{\sf E}^{(2)}
can be rewritten as

(7.12)

\check{\sf E}^{(2)}_{\,c}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\Big[(d-4)(\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}\nabla_{a}F_{bc}+3H^{g}F_{\hat{n}c})-\nabla^{a}F_{ac}\Big]^{\top}\,.

We now need to show that

\check{\sf E}^{(2)}_{\,b}(\Omega^{2}g,A)=\Omega^{-2}\check{\sf E}^{(2)}_{\,b}(g,A)\,.

The proof is then an elementary application of the transformation formula for Levi-Civita connections of conformally related metrics acting on covectors
given in Equation (
).
Also one needs that the unit normals and mean curvatures of conformally related metrics obey

\hat{n}^{\Omega^{2}g}=\Omega{\hskip 0.85358pt}\hat{n}\>\mbox{ and }\>H^{\Omega^{2}g}=\Omega^{-1}\big(H^{g}+g(\Upsilon,\hat{n})\big)\,.

That computation is somewhat tedious, a quickfire proof employs the linearized conformal variations of Section
2.2
.
That requires only application of Equation (
) to the two connections appearing in Equation (
7.12
),
as well as Equation (
2.6
) to the mean curvature.
∎

When
d\neq 5
, we may define

{\sf E}^{(2)}:=\tfrac{1}{d-5}{\hskip 0.85358pt}\check{\sf E}^{(2)}_{a}\,.

In
d=5
dimensions, only the boundary divergence term
\bar{\jmath}_{c}=\bar{\nabla}^{a}\bar{F}_{ac}
remains in
\check{\sf E}^{(2)}_{c}
,
and in
that case
\bar{\jmath}
is the conformally invariant Yang–Mills current. Theorem
1.11
established that
\bar{\jmath}
is the
d=5
obstruction current.
This is a direct analog of the case
w=(3-d)/2
for
\check{\delta}^{(2)}
acting on scalar densities in Proposition (
5.4
).

Analogy with the scalar operator
\delta^{(2_{3})}
suggests that when
d=5
and
\bar{\jmath}=0
, we ought consider the tensor

(7.13)

{\sf E}^{(2_{5})}_{\,c}(g,A):=\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}(\nabla_{a}F_{bc})|_{\Sigma}+2H^{g}{\sf E}^{(1)}_{c}\,.

Indeed a simple computation establishes that

\Omega^{2}{\hskip 0.85358pt}{\sf E}^{(2_{5})}_{\,b}(\Omega^{2}g,A)-{\sf E}^{(2_{5})}_{\,b}(g,A)=\Upsilon^{a}\bar{F}_{ab}\,.

Hence, if the boundary connection
\bar{A}

is flat, then the obstruction current
\bar{\jmath}_{b}=\bar{\nabla}^{a}\bar{F}_{ab}=0
,
and moreover
{\sf E}^{(2_{5})}(g,A)
defines a section of
T^{*}\Sigma\otimes{\mathcal{V}}\Sigma[-2]
.
In Section
7.3

we establish that this tensor invariantly captures the Neumann data for global solutions to the
d=5
conformally compact Yang–Mills system whose boundary Dirichlet data is a flat connection
\bar{A}
. Note that since
{\sf E}^{(2_{5})}
has weight
-2
, its divergence is conformally invariant.
In fact we have the following result.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
(M,\boldsymbol{c},\sigma)
be a dimension
d=5
Poincaré–Einstein structure. If
\nabla^{A}
is a conformally compact Yang–Mills connection on
{\mathcal{V}}M
for which
F^{A}|_{\Sigma}=0
, then

\bar{\nabla}^{a}{\sf E}^{(2_{5})}_{a}=0\,.

Proof.

Firstly observe that, by virtue of the Noether identity
\nabla^{a}\nabla^{b}F_{ab}=0
and the symmetry property
\nabla_{a}n_{b}=\nabla_{b}n_{a}
, the divergence of the conformally compact Yang–Mills condition
\sigma\nabla^{a}F_{ab}=(d-4)F_{nb}
gives

n^{b}\nabla^{a}F_{ab}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}(d-4)n^{a}\nabla^{b}F_{ab}\,.

Thus, since
d\neq 3
, we have

n_{c}\nabla_{b}F^{bc}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}0\,.

In turn

\bar{\nabla}^{a}{\sf E}^{(2_{5})}_{a}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\nabla_{c}^{\top}\big(\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}(\nabla_{a}F_{b}{}^{c})+2H^{g}F_{\hat{n}}{}^{c}\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\nabla_{c}^{\top}(\nabla_{b}F^{bc}-\nabla^{\top}_{b}F^{bc})\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}{\hskip 0.85358pt}\hat{n}_{c}\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla_{b}F^{bc}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}(\nabla_{\hat{n}}-H)(n_{c}\nabla_{b}F^{bc})=0\,.\>\>

In the above we again used the Noether identity, the vanishing condition
F^{A}|_{\Sigma}=0
, as well as Equation (
5.17
).
∎

Finally for the
k=2
case, in dimensions
d>5
, as promised we have the following.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
(M,\boldsymbol{c},\sigma)
be a dimension
d\geq 6
Poincaré–Einstein structure. If
\nabla^{A}
is a conformally compact Yang–Mills connection on
{\mathcal{V}}M
, then

{\sf E}^{(2)}_{a}=0\,.

Proof.

Since the conformally compact Yang–Mills condition implies
{\sf E}^{(1)}

=

0
, we have

{\sf E}^{(2)}_{b}=\hat{n}^{a}\nabla_{\hat{n}}F_{ab}|_{\Sigma}-\tfrac{1}{d-5}{\hskip 0.85358pt}{\bar{\jmath}}_{b}\,.

The result now follows from direct application of Equation (
5.30
).
∎

7.2.1.
Higher Order Boundary Operators

We are now ready to construct
{\sf E}^{(3)}
and
{\sf E}^{(4)}
.
Starting with the former we seek an operator that takes three transverse derivatives of the connection potential. This operator probes Neumann data
in dimension
d=6
and is required to vanish on conformally compact Yang–Mills connections in dimensions
d\geq 7
.

Proposition 7.4
.

Let
(M,\boldsymbol{c},\sigma)
be a dimension Poincaré–Einstein structure
equipped with a smooth connection
\nabla^{A}
on
{\mathcal{V}}M
. Then, when
d\neq 7
,

{\sf E}^{(3)}_{c}(g,A):=\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}([\nabla_{\hat{n}}+5H]\nabla_{a}F_{bc})|_{\Sigma}+\tfrac{2}{d-1}\bar{\nabla}_{c}\bar{\nabla}^{a}{\sf E}_{a}^{(1)}+2\bar{P}^{a}{}_{c}{\sf E}_{a}^{(1)}-\tfrac{3}{2}\bar{J}{\sf E}_{c}^{(1)}\\[2.84526pt]
+(5H^{2}-2P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}){\sf E}_{c}^{(1)}-(\bar{\nabla}^{a}H)\bar{F}_{ac}-\tfrac{3}{d-7}\boxast{\sf E}_{c}^{(1)}

defines a section of
T^{*}\Sigma\otimes{\mathcal{V}}\Sigma[-3]
.

When
d=7
and
\nabla^{A}
is subject to
{\sf E}^{(1)}=0
,

{\sf E}^{(3_{7})}_{c}(g,A):=\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}(\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla_{a}F_{bc})|_{\Sigma}+\tfrac{5}{2}H(\check{\sf E}_{c}^{(2)}+\bar{\jmath}_{c})-(\bar{\nabla}^{a}H)\bar{F}_{ac}

defines a section of
T^{*}\Sigma\otimes{\mathcal{V}}\Sigma[-3]
.

Remark 7.5
.

In dimensions
d\neq 5,7
, the operator
{\sf E}^{(3)}
has the simpler expression

(7.14)

{\sf E}^{(3)}_{c}(g,A)=\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}(\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla_{a}F_{bc})|_{\Sigma}+5H({\sf E}_{c}^{(2)}+\tfrac{1}{d-5}{\hskip 0.85358pt}\bar{\jmath}_{c})\\[2.84526pt]
-(5H^{2}+2P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}){\sf E}_{c}^{(1)}-(\bar{\nabla}^{a}H)\bar{F}_{ac}-\tfrac{3}{d-7}\boxast_{7}{\sf E}_{c}^{(1)}\,.

Proof of Proposition
7.4
.

The above operators were constructed in the first instance by teaching the symbolic manipulation software FORM
[
46
]
both the conformal transformation properties and identities obeyed by Yang–Mills coupled hypersurface invariants, and thereafter fitting coefficients for the most general expression whose leading term is a non-zero multiple of
\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}(\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla_{a}F_{bc})|_{\Sigma}
and
obeys

{\sf E}^{(3)}_{\,a}(\Omega^{2}g,A)=\Omega^{-3}{\sf E}^{(3)}_{\,a}(g,A)\,.

Once one has the given operators at hand, it is easy to prove the proposition statement by considering their linearized conformal variations.
(We used the full, rather than linearized, conformal transformations in the symbolic software computation as a check of that work, which by itself also constitutes a proof of the claimed result.)

A short computation establishes that the linearized conformal variation of the leading order term in
{\sf E}^{(3)}
, when
d\neq 5
,
is given by

\displaystyle\delta_{-3}\big(\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}(\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla_{a}F_{bc})|_{\Sigma}\big)

\displaystyle=(\bar{\nabla}^{a}\Upsilon_{\hat{n}})\bar{F}_{ac}-6H\bar{F}_{\bar{\Upsilon}c}-2\bar{\Upsilon}^{a}\bar{\nabla}_{c}^{\phantom{(1)}\!\!\!\!}{\sf E}^{(1)}_{a}+3\bar{\nabla}_{\bar{\Upsilon}}{\sf E}^{(1)}_{c}

\displaystyle\hskip 48.36958pt-2\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}(\nabla_{a}\Upsilon_{b}){\sf E}^{(1)}_{c}+10H\Upsilon_{\hat{n}}{\sf E}^{(1)}_{c}-\tfrac{5}{d-5}\Upsilon_{\hat{n}}\big(\check{\sf E}^{(2)}_{c}+\bar{\jmath}_{c}\big)\,.

For
{\sf E}^{(3_{7})}
one can strike off the third through sixth terms on the right hand side above.

The stated invariance properties now follow almost by inspection if one employs the linearized conformal variations given in Section
2.2
. For instance, when
d\neq 5
,

\delta_{-3}\Big(\tfrac{5}{d-5}H\bar{\jmath}_{c}-(\bar{\nabla}^{a}H)\bar{F}_{ac}\Big)=\tfrac{5}{d-5}\big((\delta_{-1}H)\bar{\jmath}_{c}+H\delta_{-2}\bar{\jmath}_{c}\big)-(\bar{\nabla}^{a}\delta_{-1}H-\bar{\Upsilon}^{a}H)\bar{F}_{ac}\\
=\tfrac{5}{d-5}\Upsilon_{\hat{n}}\bar{\jmath}_{c}+6H\bar{F}_{\bar{\Upsilon}c}-(\bar{\nabla}^{a}\Upsilon_{\hat{n}})\bar{F}_{ac}\,,\hskip 98.16191pt

which yields terms canceling the first, second and last terms on the right hand side of the preceding display.
The remaining cancellations are not more difficult to establish than these ones. We leave the special
d=5,7
cases to the reader.
∎

Remark 7.6
.

Theorem
1.2
and Corollary
4.2

established that smoothness of conformally compact Yang–Mills connections is obstructed at order
d-4
by the obstruction current
\bar{k}
. This is the underlying reason why we introduced the boundary operator
\check{\sf E}^{(2)}
with a factor
(d-5)
multiplying the leading transverse derivative term
\hat{n}^{a}(\nabla_{\hat{n}}F_{ab})|_{\Sigma}
. One might have expected the same behavior for the third order operator
{\sf E}^{(3)}
involving a factor of
(d-6)
. However,
for the special case of Poincaré–Einstein structures, the
d=6
obstruction current
\bar{k}
vanishes; see Theorem
1.11
. Instead
we encounter a
d=7
pole in
{\sf E}^{(3)}
whose residue
-3\boxast{\sf E}^{(1)}
is conformally invariant.
In
d=7
dimensions, invariance of the boundary operator
{\sf E}^{(3_{7})}
is
conditioned
on vanishing of
{\sf E}^{(1)}
. The operator
{\sf E}^{(3_{7})}
then probes the term in the connection of one lower order than that of the obstruction.
This
phenomenon mirrors exactly that observed in Remark
5.5
for the scalar operator
\check{\delta}^{(3)}
and
has a direct analog in the theory of conformal fundamental forms developed in
[
6
]
for the study of obstructions to conformally compact structures being
Poincaré–Einstein.
\blacklozenge

In
d=6
dimensions, the operator
{\sf E}^{(3)}
accesses the Neumann data of a conformally compact Yang–Mills connection. Necessarily—see Section
7.3
—this must be encoded by a divergence-free boundary covector.

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
(M,\boldsymbol{c},\sigma)
be a dimension
d=6
Poincaré–Einstein structure. Then, if
\nabla^{A}
is a
conformally compact Yang–Mills connection on
{\mathcal{V}}M
,
we have

\bar{\nabla}^{a}{\sf E}^{(3)}_{a}=0\,.

Proof.

Firstly observe that the asymptotically conformally compact Yang–Mills condition

(7.15)

\sigma\nabla^{a}F_{ab}-2F_{nb}=0\,,

here implies that

{\sf E}^{(1)}=0={\sf E}^{(2)}\,.

The first of the above equalities is a direct consequence of the definition
{\sf E}^{(1)}_{b}:=\hat{n}^{a}F_{ab}|_{\Sigma}
, while the second
was established in Lemma
7.3
.

Next we note that contracting (
7.15
)
with
n^{b}
gives

\sigma n^{b}\nabla^{a}F_{ab}=0\,,

and thus, away from
\Sigma
—and in turn by smoothness of
A
everywhere in
M
—we have

(7.16)

n^{b}\nabla^{a}F_{ab}=\nabla^{a}F_{an}=0\,.

The proof strategy is now to successively apply powers of
\nabla_{n}
to the above display. Indeed

0=\nabla_{n}^{2}\nabla^{a}F_{an}=\nabla^{a}(\nabla_{n}^{2}F_{an})-[\nabla^{a},\nabla_{n}^{2}]F_{an}\\
=\nabla^{a}\big((n^{d}\nabla_{n}^{2}+[\nabla_{n}^{2},n^{d}])F_{ad}\big)-(\nabla^{a}n^{b})\nabla_{b}\nabla_{n}F_{an}\\
-[F^{a}{}_{n},\nabla_{n}F_{an}]-R^{a}{}_{na}{}^{d}\nabla_{n}F_{dn}-\nabla_{n}([\nabla^{a},\nabla_{n}]F_{an})\,.

We proceed computing along
\Sigma
. For that we may repeatedly use
0={\sf E}^{(1)}_{a}=F_{na}|_{\Sigma}
as well as Equation
5.30
to give
\nabla_{n}F_{na}|_{\Sigma}=\bar{\jmath}_{a}
.
Hence

0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\nabla^{a}\big(n^{c}n^{d}\nabla_{n}\nabla_{c}F_{ad}+n^{d}(\nabla_{n}n^{c})\nabla_{c}F_{ad}\big)+\nabla^{a}\big((\nabla_{n}n^{d})\nabla_{n}F_{ad}+\nabla_{n}((\nabla_{n}n^{d})F_{ad})\big)\\
-H\nabla^{a}\nabla_{n}F_{an}-4(\bar{\nabla}^{d}H){\hskip 0.85358pt}\bar{\jmath}_{d}-\nabla_{\hat{n}}\big(R^{a}{}_{na}{}^{d}F_{dn}+(\nabla^{a}n^{b})\nabla_{b}F_{an}\big)\,.

In the above we also used Equation (
5.8
).
Computing further along the same lines, but now using Equations
(
5.16
), (
5.17
), (
5.31
) and (
5.32
), gives

0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\nabla_{a}^{\top}(\hat{n}^{c}\hat{n}^{d}\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla_{c}F^{a}{}_{d})+\hat{n}^{a}\nabla_{\hat{n}}(n^{c}n^{d}\nabla_{n}\nabla_{c}F_{ad})+\hat{n}^{d}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}(\nabla^{a}\nabla^{c}n^{2})\nabla_{c}F_{ad}+H\hat{n}^{c}\nabla^{a}\nabla_{c}F_{ad}\big)\\
+2(\nabla^{a}\nabla_{n}n^{d})(\hat{n}_{a}\bar{\jmath}_{d}-\hat{n}_{d}\bar{\jmath}_{a}-2H\bar{F}_{ad})+2H\hat{n}^{d}\nabla^{a}\nabla_{n}F_{ad}\hskip 65.44142pt\\
+(\nabla^{a}\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla_{n}n^{d})\bar{F}_{ad}+2(\nabla_{n}^{2}n^{d})\,\bar{\jmath}_{d}+H\bar{\nabla}^{a}\bar{\jmath}_{a}-H\hat{n}^{a}\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla_{n}F_{an}\\
-8(\bar{\nabla}^{d}H){\hskip 0.85358pt}\bar{\jmath}_{d}-(\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla^{a}n^{b})\nabla_{b}F_{an}-H\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla^{a}F_{an}\,.\hskip 56.9055pt

We now develop each non-trivial term above
(the key identities underlying the following list of results can be found in Section
5
, and in particular Subsection
5.3
)

\displaystyle\hat{n}^{a}\nabla_{\hat{n}}(n^{c}n^{d}\nabla_{n}\nabla_{c}F_{ad})\!\!

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{c}(\nabla_{\hat{n}}n^{d})\nabla_{n}\nabla_{c}F_{ad}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}H\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{c}\hat{n}^{d}\nabla_{n}\nabla_{c}F_{ad}=0\,,

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\hat{n}^{d}(\nabla^{a}\nabla^{c}n^{2})\nabla_{c}F_{ad}\!\!

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle-\hat{n}^{d}(\nabla^{a}\nabla^{c}(\rho\sigma))\nabla_{c}F_{ad}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}H\hat{n}^{d}(\nabla^{a}n^{c})\nabla_{c}F_{ad}-\hat{n}^{d}(\nabla^{a}\rho)\nabla_{\hat{n}}F_{ad}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle 2H^{2}\hat{n}^{d}\bar{\jmath}_{d}+\hat{n}^{d}(\bar{\nabla}^{a}H)\nabla_{\hat{n}}F_{ad}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}-(\bar{\nabla}^{a}H)\bar{\jmath}_{a}\,,

\displaystyle H\hat{n}^{d}\hat{n}^{c}\nabla^{a}\nabla_{c}F_{ad}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle H\hat{n}^{d}\hat{n}^{c}\nabla_{a}^{\top}\nabla_{c}F^{a}{}_{d}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}H\hat{n}^{d}\hat{n}^{c}\nabla_{a}^{\top}\nabla^{\top}_{c}\bar{F}^{a}{}_{d}\!+\!H\hat{n}^{d}\hat{n}^{c}\nabla_{a}^{\top}(\hat{n}_{c}\nabla_{\hat{n}}F^{a}{}_{d})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle-H^{2}\hat{n}^{d}\nabla_{a}^{\top}\bar{F}^{a}{}_{d}+H\hat{n}_{d}\nabla_{a}^{\top}\nabla_{\hat{n}}F^{ad}=-H\bar{\nabla}_{a}\bar{\jmath}^{a}=0\,,

\displaystyle 2(\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla_{n}n^{d})\bar{\jmath}_{d}\!\!

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle 2(\bar{\nabla}^{d}H)\bar{\jmath}_{d}\,,

\displaystyle-2\bar{\jmath}^{a}\hat{n}_{d}\nabla_{a}^{\top}\nabla_{n}n^{d}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle-2(\bar{\nabla}^{d}H)\bar{\jmath}_{d}\,,

\displaystyle 2H\hat{n}^{d}\nabla^{a}\nabla_{n}F_{ad}\!\!

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle 2H\hat{n}_{d}\nabla_{a}^{\top}\nabla_{n}F^{ad}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}-2H\bar{\nabla}^{a}\bar{\jmath}_{a}=0\,,

\displaystyle-4H\bar{F}_{ad}\nabla^{a}\nabla_{n}n^{d}\!\!

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle 0\,,

\displaystyle\bar{F}^{ad}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla_{n}n_{d}\!\!

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle 0\,,

\displaystyle 2\bar{j}^{d}\nabla_{n}^{2}n_{d}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle 2(\bar{\nabla}^{d}H)\bar{\jmath}_{d}\,,

\displaystyle H\bar{\nabla}^{a}\bar{\jmath}_{a}\!\!

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle 0\,,

\displaystyle-H\hat{n}^{a}\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla_{n}F_{an}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle-H[n^{a},\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla_{n}]F_{an}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}H^{2}\hat{n}^{a}\nabla_{\hat{n}}F_{an}+H\nabla_{\hat{n}}\big(\nabla^{a}(-\rho\sigma)F_{an}\big)\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}\!0\,,

\displaystyle-(\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla^{a}n^{b})\nabla_{b}F_{an}\!\!

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle(P^{ab}+g^{ab}P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}})\nabla_{b}F_{an}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}(P^{ab}+g^{ab}P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}})\hat{n}_{b}\nabla_{\hat{n}}F_{an}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}(\bar{\nabla}^{d}H)\bar{\jmath}_{d}\,,

\displaystyle-H\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla^{a}F_{an}\!\!

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}H\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla^{a}(\sigma\nabla^{b}F_{ba})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}-\frac{1}{2}H\nabla_{\hat{n}}(n^{a}\nabla^{b}F_{ba})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}0\,.

Orchestrating gives

0=\nabla^{\top}_{c}(\hat{n}_{a}\hat{n}_{b}\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla^{a}F^{bc})+6(\bar{\nabla}_{c}H)\bar{\jmath}^{c}=\bar{\nabla}^{a}{\sf E}^{(3)}_{a}\,.

The second equality above used Equation (
7.14
) with

{\sf E}^{(1)}
and
{\sf E}^{(2)}
set to zero, as well as the (boundary) Noether identity.
∎

We also stipulated at the beginning of this section that the boundary operators
{\sf E}^{(3)}
and
{\sf E}^{(3_{7})}
obey vanishing properties when evaluated on conformally compact Yang–Mills connections in dimensions
d\geq 7
. Such is given below.

Lemma 7.8
.

Let
(M,\boldsymbol{c},\sigma)
be a dimension
d\geq 7
Poincaré–Einstein structure. If
\nabla^{A}
is a
conformally compact Yang–Mills connection on
{\mathcal{V}}M
then

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}{\sf E}^{(3_{7})}_{a}=0\,,\qquad d=7\,,\\
\,{\sf E}^{(3)}_{a}\,=0\,,\qquad\,d\geq 8\,.\end{array}\right.

Proof.

From Lemma
7.3
we have

{\sf E}^{(2)}=0={\sf E}^{(1)}
so (for
d\geq 8
)

{\sf E}^{(3)}_{a}=\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}(\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla_{a}F_{bc})|_{\Sigma}+\tfrac{5}{d-5}\big(H\bar{\jmath}_{c}+(1-\tfrac{d}{5})(\bar{\nabla}^{a}H)\bar{F}_{ac}\big)\,.

The same expression applies for
{\sf E}^{(3_{7})}_{a}
when
d=7
.
The result now follows by applying Equation (
5.34
) (remembering that
P_{\hat{n}a}^{\top}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{=}}-\bar{\nabla}_{a}H
; see Equation (
5.8
)).
∎

The final case we consider is the transverse order four boundary operator.
As it takes four transverse derivatives of the potential, this operator will probe Neumann data
when
d=7
and is required to vanish on conformally compact Yang–Mills connections in dimensions
d\geq 8
. Because the critical dimension
d=7
is odd, we expect it to exhibit special behavior relative to its
{\sf E}^{(3)}
counterpart, but closer to its
{\sf E}^{(2)}
companion.

Proposition 7.9
.

Let
(M,\boldsymbol{c},\sigma)
be a dimension
d\neq 4,5,7,9
Poincaré–Einstein structure
equipped with a smooth connection
\nabla^{A}
on
{\mathcal{V}}M
. Then

\displaystyle\check{\sf E}^{(4)}_{d}(g,A)

\displaystyle:=(d-7)\Big[\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}\hat{n}^{c}(\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}F_{cd})|_{\Sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+9H\big({\sf E}^{(3)}_{d}+\tfrac{3}{d-7}\boxast_{7}{\sf E}_{d}^{(1)}\big)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-(7P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}+\tfrac{45}{2}H^{2})\big({\sf E}^{(2)}_{d}+\tfrac{1}{d-5}\bar{\jmath}_{d}\big)+(\bar{\nabla}^{a}H)\big(3\bar{\nabla}_{d}{\sf E}^{(1)}_{a}-4\bar{\nabla}_{a}{\sf E}^{(1)}_{d}\big)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\big((d+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}14\color[rgb]{0,0,0})H^{3}+2(d+6)HP_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}H-2(\bar{\Delta}H)-2H\bar{J}-2\nabla_{\hat{n}}J\big){\sf E}^{(1)}_{d}

(7.17)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+\big((\bar{\nabla}^{a}P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}})+9H(\bar{\nabla}^{a}H)\big)\bar{F}_{ad}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+\tfrac{8}{d-4}\big[{\sf E}^{(1)}_{d},\bar{\nabla}^{a}{\sf E}^{(1)}_{a}\big]+\tfrac{12}{(d-5)(d-9)}[\bar{F}_{ad},\bar{\jmath}^{a}]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\tfrac{6}{d-9}\Big(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boxast_{9}\!\big({\sf E}^{(2)}_{d}+\tfrac{1}{d-5}\bar{\jmath}_{d}\big)+\bar{P}^{ab}\bar{\nabla}_{a}\bar{F}_{bd}+\tfrac{1}{2}(\bar{\nabla}^{a}\bar{J})\bar{F}_{ad}+\tfrac{1}{2}\bar{C}_{abd}\bar{F}^{ab}\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\phantom{:=}+\tfrac{12}{d-9}\hat{k}_{d}\,,

where

\displaystyle\hat{k}_{b}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\quad\tfrac{1}{2}\bar{\nabla}^{a}\Big(\bar{\nabla}_{[a}{\bar{\jmath}}_{b]}-4\bar{P}_{[a}{}^{c}\bar{F}_{b]c}-\bar{J}\bar{F}_{ab}\Big)+\tfrac{1}{4}[\bar{\jmath}^{a},\bar{F}_{ab}]\,,

defines a section of
T^{*}\Sigma\otimes{\mathcal{V}}\Sigma[-4]
.

Proof.

Once again, the expression
7.17
was generated by the symbolic manipulation software FORM so that the same remarks as made at the beginning of the proof of Proposition
7.4
apply here, and
we shall rely on the linearized conformal variations developed in Section (
2.2
)
to show that

\check{\sf E}^{(4)}_{\,a}(\Omega^{2}g,A)=\Omega^{-4}\check{\sf E}^{(4)}_{\,a}(g,A)\,.

The most difficult of those variations is that of the first term in Equation (
7.17
). This is given by

\displaystyle\delta_{-4}\big(\hat{n}^{a}

\displaystyle\hat{n}^{b}\hat{n}^{c}(\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}F_{cd})|_{\Sigma}\big)\>=\>\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!-9\Upsilon_{\hat{n}}\big({\sf E}^{(3)}_{d}+\tfrac{3}{d-7}\boxast_{7}{\sf E}^{(1)}_{d}\big)\!+\!21H\bar{\Upsilon}^{a}\bar{\nabla}_{d}^{\phantom{(1)}\!\!\!\!}{\sf E}^{(1)}_{a}\!-31H\bar{\nabla}_{\bar{\Upsilon}}{\sf E}^{(1)}_{d}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-\big(7\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}(\nabla_{a}\Upsilon_{b})-45H\Upsilon_{\hat{n}}\big)\big({\sf E}^{(2)}_{d}+\tfrac{1}{d-5}\bar{\jmath}_{d}\big)\!+\!9(P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}+\tfrac{7}{2}H^{2})\bar{F}_{\bar{\Upsilon}d}-9H(\bar{\nabla}^{a}\Upsilon_{\hat{n}})\bar{F}_{ad}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+\big(\bar{\nabla}^{a}(\hat{n}^{b}\nabla_{\hat{n}}\Upsilon_{b})\big)\bar{F}_{ad}-9\Upsilon_{\hat{n}}(\bar{\nabla}^{a}H)\bar{F}_{ad}-(\bar{\nabla}^{a}\Upsilon_{\hat{n}})\big(3\bar{\nabla}_{d}{\sf E}^{(1)}_{a}-4\bar{\nabla}_{a}{\sf E}^{(1)}_{d}\big)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+(\bar{\nabla}_{d}H){\sf E}^{(1)}_{\bar{\Upsilon}}+2\bar{\Upsilon}_{d}(\bar{\nabla}^{a}H){\sf E}^{(1)}_{a}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

(7.18)

\displaystyle-\big(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}\hat{n}^{c}(\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\Upsilon_{c})-14H\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}(\nabla_{a}\Upsilon_{b})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+9(\bar{\nabla}_{\bar{\Upsilon}}H)+18\Upsilon_{\hat{n}}P_{\hat{n}\hat{n}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+45\Upsilon_{\hat{n}}H^{2}\big){\sf E}^{(1)}_{d}

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-8\big[{\sf E}^{(1)}_{d},{\sf E}^{(1)}_{\bar{\Upsilon}}\big]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+6\Big(\bar{\nabla}_{\bar{\Upsilon}}\big({\sf E}^{(2)}_{d}+\tfrac{1}{d-5}\bar{\jmath}_{d}\big)-\tfrac{1}{2}\bar{\Upsilon}^{a}\bar{\nabla}_{d}^{\phantom{(1)}\!\!\!\!}\big({\sf E}^{(2)}_{a}+\tfrac{1}{d-5}\bar{\jmath}_{a}\big)\Big)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+3\bar{P}_{d}{}^{a}\bar{F}_{\bar{\Upsilon}a}+6\bar{P}_{\bar{\Upsilon}}{}^{a}\bar{F}_{ad}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,.

While the above is tedious to compute, all that is needed is
\delta F_{cd}=0
, the result in Equation (
) for the variation of connections, and
\delta_{1}\hat{n}=0
. Thereafter one can employ the identities in Section
5
to decompose the result into intrinsic and extrinsic tensor quantities, as well as the expressions above for
{\sf E}^{(1)}
,
{\sf E}^{(2)}
,
{\sf E}^{(3)}
, and that for the operator
\boxast_{7}
.

To see that the above-displayed variation cancels that of the remaining terms in Equation (
7.17
), we begin by noting that the first three lines of the above display
exactly cancel nearly all the variations all the remaining terms in
the first two lines of (

)
as well as that of the first term on the fourth line; this is easily established using Equations (

), (

), (

), and (

The unaccounted-for variations on the first, second and fourth lines in Equation (

) come from

\delta_{-1}\big(3\bar{\nabla}_{d}{\sf E}^{(1)}_{a}-4\bar{\nabla}_{a}{\sf E}^{(1)}_{d}\big)=-2\bar{\Upsilon}_{d}{\sf E}^{(1)}_{a}+5\bar{\Upsilon}_{a}{\sf E}^{(1)}_{d}-\bar{g}_{ad}{\sf E}^{(1)}_{\bar{\Upsilon}}\,.

The first and last terms on the right hand side
are as required to cancel the two terms on the fourth line of the variation of
\hat{n}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}\hat{n}^{c}(\nabla_{\hat{n}}\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}F_{cd})|_{\Sigma}
given above.

There are three further variations unaccounted for in the second and fourth lines of (

according to (

At this point we can cancel all remaining variations proportional to one power of

It remains to cancel variations

the last two lines of (

For that we employ the variations displayed in Equation (

) as well as the variation of the last term proportional to
\hat{k}
is given in (
